A Run read on its own could not say what was sent, how long it took or what it cost.

Decided: every HTTP adapter records an `Exchange` on the reply it returns, and the loop writes what a Run owns onto its own events. The Exchange is the provider, the endpoint (base URL and path, never a key), the wire id, the sampling (the request body with the prompt keys removed, so a field nobody enumerated is still recorded), the message and tool counts with a sha256 of each, the wall milliseconds, the attempts the retry loop made, the final status and the provider's request id from `x-request-id`, `request-id` or `cf-ray`. The hash is written in `provider.py` rather than imported from `records.py`, because `ai` imports nothing of ours (D121). `ModelConfig` gains `logprobs` and `top_logprobs`: chat completions takes both, the Responses API takes `top_logprobs` plus an `include` entry, Anthropic takes neither and is sent neither, and whatever comes back stays in the reply's `raw`. What stays the same: the retry rules and everything `post` did for its callers, the `env_error` class the Verdict reads, and the offline models, which record no exchange because they never touch a wire (a memo hit carries the original call's).

- `kullback/ai/provider.py`: `Exchange`, `ModelReply.exchange`, `ModelConfig.logprobs` and `top_logprobs`, `body_hash`, `request_id_of`, `Posted`, `post` delegating to `_post_full`, `ProviderError.attempts` set by the retry loop, the logprobs fields in the two OpenAI bodies.
- `kullback/runner/loop.py`: the `model_call` event's `Cost` filled with the provider, the wall time, and the price and price source from `budget`; the error event carrying a provider failure's status and attempts; the stop event's payload carrying the system prompt and the tool specs the Candidate was given, beside the Start and End state that are already there for the same reason.
- Measured: a Run now says what was sent (sampling, prompt fingerprints, system prompt, tools), how long it took (wall milliseconds and attempts per call), and what it cost (dollars and the price source on the event itself).
### D160. The judge model is chosen, named, and may be two different models (2026-09-07)

Founder, 2026-09-07: "If your reward is semantic, use an LLM judge: I mean yes, we should do this and expose different models to be our judge model as well." Until now a build judged with the model that built it (`models["reference_judge"]` was wrapped straight off `--model`), `kullback verdict` took one `--judge-model` and got its second judge by running that same adapter again under a second persona, and every ruling of both was named `<model>:a` or `<model>:a#3`. Two samples of one model split less often than two models do and split for different reasons, and nothing on disk or in the report said which two judges had parted, so the one disagreement rate could not tell a self-agreeing model from an agreeing pair.

Decided: the judge is named where the model is named. `kullback build` takes `--judge-model` and `--second-judge-model`, both `provider/model`, both optional; the judge defaults to `--model` and the second judge to the same model under the second persona, so a build with neither flag is what it was, byte for byte. Both judge models are wrapped through `budget.py` like every other stage model, so a judge on a second provider is priced and refused past the ceiling on the same terms. `kullback verdict` and `kullback regrade` take `--second-judge-model` as well, and with it judge B is a judge of its own on that model in the first persona, with the persona third sample still the tie-breaker (D97). Every `JudgeResult.judge` is `<provider/model>:<persona letter>`, so a ruling says which model made it. What stays is D110 and D92 whole: a judge decides only what code cannot, it may fail a Run and never award a pass, two judges answer every question and a split is an abstain that goes to the disagreement queue. What is measured is disagreement by model pair.

- `judge_pairs.jsonl` and the disagreement queue rows carry `judge_a_name`, `judge_b_name` and the pair name `judges`; the writer names the pair so every reader counts under the same name.
- `disagreement_stats` gains `by_pair`: per pair of judges, its pairs, disagreements and rate. Rows written before this name no pair and join none, so an older build reads as having no by-pair number rather than a zero it never measured.
- The report's disagreement section names the judge models when they differ from the build model, and prints one line per pair beneath the overall rate.
- The build's own residue judge (D111) is still one judge over the remaining End-state groups, and this decision gives it no second: `--second-judge-model` names the other side of the two-judge protocol the Verdict path runs, and is recorded in `report_config.json` so the report can say who judged.
### D162. A tool body may not memorise the recordings (2026-09-07)

Build 13: of the 150 replay calls that differed from their recording, 126 were one `KeyError` raised by the compiled body of the tool that replaces items on an order, and 22 more were the same failure seen in a later read of the same row. The body never looked the new item up in the products table of the world; it held three item ids from the recorded calls in a dict, so every other id crashed it, and the model was asked to recompile that tool 19 times in the round without converging. A body like that replays the calls it was shown and fails everything else, which is exactly the shape no per-call gate catches. A stronger model might avoid it; a code gate makes it impossible for any model, which is what the harness is for.

Decided: `compile_tools` gains a seventh per-tool ruling, `compile_tools.memorised_values`, beside `parses`, `confined`, `executes_on_s0`, `deterministic`, `non_trivial`, `replay_fidelity` and `refuses_unknown`. It parses the body with `ast` and reads every string and number the model's own methods spell out, a dict display's keys included, then refuses the body when a literal (a) has the shape the schema mined for some table's ids, (b) equals a row id the Starting state holds, in a table's own dict or nested where `schema.homes` says its rows live, or (c) equals a value a recorded call passed this tool and neither the description nor the signature names it. Strings under three characters, the empty string and numbers under ten are code, not data; the names of the world and of the signature (tables, columns, arguments) are structure and are exempt from every rule; an id pattern that accepts an ordinary word describes no shape and is not asked rule (a), since `mine.id_pattern` falls back to a bare character class when a column's values share nothing. The failure names the literal as the code holds it, the rule that caught it and the table or the argument it came from, and it leaks nothing: it is the model's own source, and rule (c) names an argument, never a call. A refused body is treated exactly like one that fails `non_trivial`: the tool is not accepted, the compile stage retries with the failure in the model's context up to `max_attempts`, the red light reads `compile_tools.memorised_values` with `repair_recompile` as its verb, and `record_tool_lesson` carries one sentence into the Builder's memory beside the mechanic's hint, that the body memorised recorded ids and the repair is the lookup over the world's tables.

- Files: `kullback/gates/tool_runs.py` (the ruling and its helpers), `kullback/gates/__init__.py` (registered, exported), `kullback/builder/sandbox.py` (`run_gates` runs it after the confinement gate, before any subprocess, and takes the tool's signature), `kullback/builder/compile_env.py` (both callers pass the signature; the compile prompt says the body reads the world's tables for every id and value), `kullback/builder/tools.py` (the verb map), `kullback/builder/repair.py` (the lesson, and the ratchet's `refused` set), `tests/gates/test_memorised_values.py`, `tests/builder/test_compile_env.py`, `tests/builder/test_repair.py`.
- What stays: the recording is still the standard (D155). A literal that is a value only the recorded results carry, a status a tool writes or a message it raises, is untouched; only ids, id shapes and recorded argument values are refused. The held-out split stays hidden: the ruling names arguments, never calls.
- What the ratchet knows: `ratchet_bodies` and `ratchet_hook` take a `refused` set and do not restore a prior body named in it. A body cleared the gates of the build it was written in, and a build that adds a gate can hold a prior body no gate accepts today; ratcheting onto it would keep a body every later round has to repair and never can.
- Known residual: an enum-shaped argument whose members the mined signature does not declare and whose description does not list is flagged by rule (c). The answer is to declare it, not to loosen the rule; the count is in the measurement below.
- Measured, per build: bodies refused by the gate, and recompiles per tool. The first is the gate's own count and the second is what build 13 spent 19 attempts of on one tool; both are read off `tool_builds.json`, which records every attempt's rulings. Run over build 13's own sixteen bodies with its schema and its Starting state, the ruling refuses one and passes the other fifteen: the one is a read that transcribed fifty of a table's rows into a literal dict instead of walking the table, which is the same defect as the one that cost the round its replays and which nothing in that round flagged.
### D164. A tool has callers, and a name the recording refused on every call is not a tool (2026-09-07)

The first telecom-domain build died in compile_tools after three attempts: 36 of 42 tools assisted. Read call by call: the simulated user in those traces runs a toolkit of its own (5,287 calls under `requestor: user` beside the assistant's 2,445), 27 tools were answered only for the user, and when the recorded agent called one of them the recording refused it with "Error: Tool 'x' not found." 21 more names were the recorded agent's own inventions (`$DEVICE_ACTION`, `$AGENT_FUNCTION{check_network_status}`, `$SYNTHETIC_TOOL_CALL`), refused on every call, none a Python identifier. The miner counted only the assistant's calls, so a user-only tool got a ToolSig built from the assistant's refusals alone, the invented names got ToolSigs too, the code-owned skeleton `def $DEVICE_ACTION(...)` did not parse (refused, "the code-owned skeleton does not parse: line 119"), and the stage failed. The compile stage meanwhile handed every call regardless of requestor to the body writer, so the bodies were written against the user's calls while the sig said otherwise. Underneath, the error classifier's tool_not_found pattern named the tool with `[\w.\-]*`, so a `$` in the name dropped the refusal to not_found_entity.

Decided: a ToolSig carries `callers`, the requestors the recording answered (any call not refused as tool_not_found), and `refused_callers`, the requestors it refused on every call. Mining takes every requestor's calls into the ToolSig of the tool it calls (schema, samples, error shapes); the customer's system stays what the assistant saw (schema, effects, Starting state, R33 of the cross-domain check). A name with no caller at all, or one that cannot be a Python identifier, gets no ToolSig: `unknown_tools.json` lists it with its reason and the mine gate reports the count, never a failure, because the Router already answers a name no tool has with tool_not_found. The compile stage gives a body only its callers' calls. The Router takes the requestor with every call and refuses a call from a requestor that is not a caller with tool_not_found before any code tool, recording or stand-in is consulted; the replay's user turns already pass `user` (D71). The classifier's tool name token is anything but a quote or whitespace. Airline and retail carry no user calls and no invented names, so nothing moves there.

What this leaves open is D71's first part, now with numbers: the user's own tools need a world to act on (the phone's state) and R33 keeps the user's results out of the Starting state, so their bodies read state the world does not hold; 9 of the 27 user-only tools failed replay_fidelity on exactly that (a status text that named airplane mode where the recording had data enabled). See Pending.

### D165. The non_trivial gate reads a tool whose answer changed under identical arguments as state-driven (2026-09-07)

The same build: four argument-free tools failed non_trivial with "the body answers every call the same way" on every attempt, with 2 to 24 distinct recorded answers over calls whose arguments were all `{}`. The recorded answer changed because a write earlier in the same recording changed the world, and the gate runs every call on one world, so a right body can only give one answer there.

Decided: the gate groups the recorded successful answers by argument set; if any argument set holds more than one distinct canonical answer, the ruling passes with `state_driven: true` and replay_fidelity, which runs the calls in sequence, is the judge. Otherwise the rule stands: different arguments the recording answered differently must get different answers. A constant body for a state-driven tool now fails at replay_fidelity with the call named, which is the failure the compiler can act on.

### D167. An id shape is a pattern an ordinary word cannot match (2026-09-07)

The first airline-domain build: five of thirteen tools stayed assisted for four attempts on the memorised_values gate (D162) alone, refusing the literals 'amount' (a dict key), 'landed' (a status word), 'Dallas' and 'Denver' as memorised ids. The miner had given the reservation ids, six characters mixing letters and digits at every position, the pattern `^.{6}$`, and `_shaped` let it through because its three probes ("value", "name", "text") are not six letters long. One refusal was right ('HATHAT' is a reservation id) and the rest cost the tools their attempts.

Decided, in two places. The shape miner emits a character class for a position that mixes letters and digits (`[A-Z0-9]`, `[a-z0-9]`, `[A-Za-z0-9]`) and "." only for a position holding anything else, so those ids get `^[A-Z0-9]{6}$`. The gate's shape test probes a pattern with a plain lowercase word and a Capitalised word of every length from 1 to 24 and calls shapeless any pattern that accepts one; a digits-only probe is not used, because `^\d{10}$` is a real id shape.

### D168. The body skill: what a body does, read off where bodies kept failing (2026-09-07)

Founder, on steering the model: "if you want to steer the model you can observe the fails and then write up the skill names and stuff." Four builds over three customers' traces, body by body, fail in a small number of ways that no gate names in advance: a body raised TypeError for an argument the recorded call never passed (it added a parameter of its own, three tools); a body read a column no table holds; a body answered null where the recording held a value from a related row; an appended history entry had the wrong sign, then the wrong keys, then an index past the end (four attempts on one tool); a status text put its segments in the body's own sequence; a search answered an empty list where the recording listed rows; a body bound a placeholder name copied out of a refused call's payload (the confined gate caught it); bodies answered every argument set the same way.

Decided: `kullback/builder/body_skill.py` holds the body skill, one paragraph per habit, in the system prefix of every body-writing call beside `_SYSTEM` (the same bytes for every tool of a build, so the cache prefix holds) and hashed into the compile_tools stage version so an edit to it recompiles. It names no domain, tool or value, and only gates that exist; a test checks both. Whether it works is the next builds' evidence against these: attempts per tool and the gate each attempt fell at.

### D170. Findings are filed by rule off the round's records, ranked by the Tasks they cost (2026-09-07)

Founder, reading the loop: "the builder was designed to get feedback from the examiner and improve the environment not only the intent (this is alignment problem) fix this please." The numbers, from `scripts/feedback_loop.py` over three model-driven builds: the Examiner filed 13 findings and 11 said the same thing, an Intent phrase with no grounded span. The Builder followed nearly all of them and no Task became trusted. Nothing named the losses that decide the number: one assisted seed tool left 52 Tasks with no Reference and two more left 22 and 12; of the 152 Tasks that had a Reference, second_path_passes cost 39, mutation_flips 37, unsolved_state_fails 31, empty_fails and plausible_wrong_fails 25 each, leak_check_clean 22. On one build the Examiner made 24 `finding` calls to put 7 on the list; on another it filed nothing in three rounds while 80 of 119 Tasks had no Reference. A finding the model chooses to write is a finding the model may not write.

The rule. Four kinds of finding are filed by rule off task_status.json, tool_fidelity.json and references.json, before the model's first choice and whether or not there is a model at all: one per tool whose own differing calls leave Tasks with no Reference (the Tasks it blocks, its replay counts, and the gate's first differing call quoted as the hint, which names the leaf and both values or the error class, D154); one per D79 check that failed, and a separate one per check that never ran, because a check whose gate had no input is a Task short of a Run and not a wrong Verifier; one per Task whose required atoms reject every held-out frontier Run, naming the Run and the atom; one per Task whose recordings do not settle on an End state, unless a tool that blocks it already explains it.

Blocked means blocked by the Task's own differing calls, which is D171's per-Task grain and not the corpus-level assisted mark: a rule counting every Task that calls an assisted tool would file one finding for 64 Tasks where 13 are the ones a recompile buys, and the other 51 would never get the finding their own reason deserves. The counts and the quoted difference come from `tool_fidelity.json`, so nothing parses a gate's failure line to find out which tool a ruling is about. The false-rejection rule measures over D173's pool.

The repair named is picked by who owns the artifact (D123): a leak that reached the Intent is the Builder's, a Verifier's atoms are the Examiner's, a Task with one Reference needs Runs before it needs a looser Verifier, and a disagreement is a refusal. A finding answered by the other agent's hand reaches this one as a report saying so.

Findings are ranked by the Tasks they cost, most first, and that is the order the Builder is handed them in, so a build opens on the tool blocking 52 Tasks rather than on the fifty-third Intent. A key (the kind and the thing it is about) already open is not filed again, and the model's `finding` tool refuses a duplicate with the id of the one that holds it. A key the Builder has already answered is filed again only when the Tasks it costs are a different set, which is how a repair that half worked stays visible while the same loss over the same Tasks is news the Builder has had. One round files at most ten, ranked before the cut, because each costs the Builder a turn; the ones cut are the cheapest and the next round files them once the ones above are closed.

Measured on the offline build over the three-Task fixture: it reached round 454 before the duplicate rule existed; with it, it files three findings, its code driver calls the three repair verbs they name where it used to call none, and it ends on the stalled exit in three rounds. That is the first measurement of what the founder asked for, a Builder with no model in it repairing the Environment off the Examiner's records. A workdir with no `tool_fidelity.json` files no tool finding at all rather than one attributed the old way. The counter to watch on the next builds is Tasks trusted after a round whose lead was a blocking tool.

### D171. Tool fidelity is attributed per Task as well as per corpus (2026-09-07)

Founder, reading a retail build's task_status.json: "obviously we need to attribute tool fidelity both (across the task and the corpus as well). improve this please." The numbers behind it, from that build's 205 Tasks and three assisted tools. One body replayed 239 of its 240 recorded calls and stayed assisted, because the compile_tools fidelity gate holds a body to 100% of the corpus's recorded calls; that ruling is one number for the tool and the Builder repairs against it. The Examiner then wrote the tool's name into the status of every Task whose seed Traces call it. 64 rows named an assisted tool as their blocker. Reading the replay checks call by call, 51 of those 64 Tasks make no call of any assisted tool that the body answers differently from the recording: their own calls all replay exactly and their real reason sits later in the same sentence ("recordings disagree on the End state", "the judge failed every End state"). 13 Tasks have a differing own call, spread over 15 (Task, tool) pairs.

Two things were wrong, and only one of them was what the reading suggested. The corpus-level assisted mark never withheld a Reference on its own: the replay stage runs every Trace through the compiled bodies, an assisted tool included, so a Task whose calls the body answers correctly already got its Reference built on the body and no stand-in was ever wired in. What the mark did was misattribute: it put a tool's name on 51 Tasks it does not block, which sends `repair_recompile` at a body that answers those Tasks correctly and hides the reason they actually failed.

Decided: fidelity is recorded at both grains and neither replaces the other. `compile_tool` keeps one row per recorded call of the tool (`ToolBuild.call_outcomes`, written to `tool_call_outcomes.json` and into the tool's node file): the call's id, whether the kept body answered it the way the recording did, and the difference in the corpus gate's own words. The ruling per call is `body_replay_fidelity_gate`, the same one the corpus gate uses, asked about one call at a time over a single sandbox run, so a call counted replayed here is a call the corpus gate counted matched. A tool that cleared every gate replayed every call by construction and pays for no extra run. The compile_tools stage attributes those rows to Tasks through the call-id-to-Task map it already builds for D74 and releases `tool_fidelity`: `tools` is the corpus ruling per tool, `tasks` is per Task per tool how many of that Task's own recorded calls replayed, how many differed, and the first few differences. Every status row carries `tool_calls_replayed` and `tool_calls_differing`, confirmed or not. A Task is blocked by a tool only when one of its own recorded calls differs, which the row names in `blocking_tools` and quotes in its `reason`; `assisted_tools` stays on the row as the corpus fact it always was, and the report puts both numbers beside each assisted tool: calls replayed of recorded, Tasks that call it, Tasks blocked by their own differing calls. The Builder's `status` says the same beside the assisted red light, so the model spends a recompile on what it buys in Tasks rather than on how many Tasks happen to call the tool.

Why this is sound: the body is exercised on exactly the calls that Task's Run makes, and the per-call rows come from replaying the kept body against those very calls. What still confirms a Reference is the replay reaching the End state, and above it the D79 suite and the held-out check still judge the Verifier derived from it. The cost is that "confirmed" now means right on this Task's calls, not right on every call in the corpus: a body that is wrong on a call no Task of the corpus makes is no longer counted against Tasks that never make it, and if the corpus later grows a Task that does make it, that Task is blocked and the earlier ones are not. The corpus gate is unchanged, so the Builder still repairs to 100% and the assisted count in the headline still says how much of the world it could not write as code.
### D172. A Task with no Reference is unfinished until it is refused (2026-09-07)

Telecom, rebuilt on D164 to D169 with every stage running and 36 bodies compiled, closed on the exit "done" after one round at fidelity 0 of 183: every tool assisted at the corpus level, no Task with a Reference, the Examiner filing nothing. D166 had stopped the same exit when the build failed a stage; this time the build succeeded and `unfinished` was read over the Tasks with a Reference alone, which was none, so D126's state held vacuously. The Builder had made 27 Intent repairs and 11 recompiles in the round, so the loop had work it could still do.

Decided: `round_counts` lists every Task of the corpus as unfinished until it is trusted and clears fidelity, or is refused. A Task with no Reference (a seed tool assisted, a disagreement nobody refused yet) is the loop's remaining work, not a Task outside it. "Done" now means the number is final; a loop that cannot move ends on stalled (D126's gate counts, or fidelity flat for k rounds, D169) or on the round cap. The fixture-driven tests that read as done after one round now run under `max_rounds=1` and exit `max_rounds`; the D166 unit keeps its meaning over a build with no Tasks at all.

Why: an exit named done at fidelity 0 is the one lie the round driver must not tell; the founder read it twice as the build finishing. Cost: builds whose Tasks cannot all be referenced run to the stall or the cap instead of one round, which is the spend the two rules were made to bound.

### D173. Three of the D79 checks were wrong about a Verifier that never writes, and the false-rejection pool counted the Runs the Reference rule had just thrown out (2026-09-07)

`docs/builds/d79-decomposition-2026-09-07.md` takes every failing Verifier of two builds apart: 45 of 140 References on the retail build, 36 of 38 on the airline one. The whole failing population is two shapes. Read-only Tasks, 27 and 29 of them, fail because a Verifier with no write has nothing the suite can falsify; single-Reference Tasks, 20 and 10, fail because a check had no input. Nothing in either build failed because a Verifier was wrong about a Run that was right, except eight held-out Runs. Four of the causes are in the checks rather than in what the derivation wrote, and they are fixed here.

`mutation_flips` (27 Tasks and 28). The `entity_count` atom of a Task whose frontier never wrote caps writes at 0, and its mutant was `max(count - 1, 0)`, the same cap. Beside it, the Hard wrapper skips every call the seed Runs only read with, so on a Run with no write call it judged nothing and returned True even when the rule was replaced with one that never holds. Decided: an atom with nothing to mutate is not a failed flip, a cap of 0 has no mutant, and the wrapper reports how many calls it judged (`judged()`) so `hard_holds` answers None for a rule that was asked about nothing. What replaces the old failure is stricter: a Verifier in which no atom at all can be mutated fails the check and the text says nothing in it can be falsified. Second-order, worth stating plainly: on a read-only Task no compiled Hard constraint is evaluated at all, so the policy is unchecked there whatever this check says.

`unsolved_state_fails` (10 and 21). The cut prefers a tool call over an assistant turn, which is right for a Task whose outcome is a write and wrong for one whose outcome is the answer. Decided: for a Verifier that requires no write the cut falls before the first assistant turn that states a fact one of its communicate atoms asks for, and before the last assistant turn when it asks for none. The first and not the last, because a recorded conversation states the facts and then repeats them in a farewell, and cutting before the farewell leaves the answer standing earlier in the Run.

`false_rejection` (50 Tasks at 1.0 on retail, 9 on airline). `legitimate_runs` counted a replay as legitimate because it was confirmed, which means the Environment reproduced it and not that it did the job. 98 of the 112 rejected retail Runs and 17 of the 22 airline ones are recordings the same round's D111 rule had discarded minutes earlier, and 39 of the 50 retail Tasks reject only Runs their own confirmation record lists as failed. Decided: the pool the number is measured over subtracts those Runs, read off `failed_recordings`. The loosening gate keeps the whole pool, because it asks what a new version may newly pass; only D133's number changes. Re-measured over both builds as the Verifiers stood on disk: 46 Tasks at 1.0 falls to 15 and 6 falls to 1, and the held-out Runs rejected fall from 129 to 19 and from 25 to 1.

`second_path_passes` (20 and 10, every one a Task with a single Reference). The check had no Run to score and was counted the same as a check that failed. Decided: nothing about trust loosens and a single-path Task stays untrusted, but the ruling says which of the two it is, the untrusted reason carries the missing input ("not run: one Reference, so there is no second path to score") instead of a bare suite failure, and the metrics carry `checks_not_run` per Task. The two ask for different repairs, more Runs of the Task against a looser Verifier, and a reader that cannot tell them apart reaches for the wrong one.

The effect, re-running the suite over both workdirs' Verifiers with the new code: `mutation_flips` falls from 27 failing Tasks to 3 on retail and from 23 to 5 on airline, `unsolved_state_fails` from 10 to 3 and from 15 to 5, and the Tasks failing at least one check from 64 to 47 and from 28 to 17. `empty_fails`, `plausible_wrong_fails` and `leak_check_clean` are unmoved on both. The three and five still failing `mutation_flips` are the Verifiers with no mutable atom at all, which is the new failure and the right one. The first three are corrections of checks that were wrong, not relaxations; the fourth is wording and a metric.

Cost: the gates package changed, so the frozen gates need re-freezing, and the Hard wrapper's text moves every per-Task cache key. Left for the derivation: a read-only Task with no communicate atom is still a Verifier of a cap and Hard atoms that the empty Run, the wrong Run and the probe all satisfy (3 Tasks and 6), and an over-strict communicate atom on a genuinely held-out anchor Run (6 of the 10 real cases on retail) is the Examiner's repair to make.

### D175. The Examiner's read answers an index for a whole file and is never longer than READ_CHARS (2026-09-07)

Retail build 14: the Examiner's context fill reached 899 percent of its window in round 1 and 900 in round 2, with three and four compactions, on 30 calls of which 5 were `read`; airline's reached 764. A `read` with no id handed the model the whole file: task_status.json is 134,000 characters on that build, references.json 360,000, and a beat that had compacted twice read nothing it had read before. The Builder's own status had been clamped since D150 (240 characters per failure, zoom for the rest); the Examiner's read had no cut at all.

Decided: a read with no id of task_status, gates, rerolls, replays or references answers an index, one line per id (for task_status: reference confirmed or not, verifier passed or not, the failed checks, the blocking tools), with a note saying to read one id for the row; and no read is longer than `READ_CHARS` (60,000 characters, which holds any one row of any kind on the builds so far, the largest being a Reference row at 5,400) with the cut named and what to read instead. The tool's description says so, which is what stops the model from asking for the file.

Why: the Examiner's beat is the round's judgement and it cannot judge what it cannot hold; a compaction in the middle of a beat is a finding lost. Cost: a model that wants every row reads them one by one, which is the number of calls the index was made to make cheap.

### D176. A requestor's prose results are read by model-written readers into rows of the one world, marked by the requestor that revealed them (2026-09-07)

Grill of 2026-09-07 night with the founder, then an experiment (`docs/builds/readers-experiment-2026-09-07.md`). The telecom-domain corpus: the simulated user runs 27 tools of its own against its device, 5,287 recorded calls, every result a prose string ("X is now ON" with a status-bar line appended, "Airplane Mode: OFF" over several lines, "Transfer successful"), 175 distinct shapes with digits masked. `extract_rows` reads dict results only and R33 skipped every non-assistant call, so the world held no device row, the 27 bodies read state the world did not have, and fidelity was 0 of 183 Tasks with every body assisted. Two designs were on the table: one world where the device is rows like any other, or the user's tools scripted from the recording with the simulated user reporting device state from its persona. Founder: "i agree with one source of truth". How the device's columns are learned: founder, "i would just expose this as a tool and let the model do it and find common schema in them."

Decided, in four parts. (1) One world and one Router. Rows a requestor other than the assistant revealed exist in the same world, one row per Task keyed by the requestor, pinned by the Task's overlay like any row, and marked `revealed_by` on their columns; the customer-facing export omits or marks them. R33 is reworded: only the assistant's calls describe the customer's system; another requestor's calls describe that requestor's own rows. (2) The columns are proposed by the model through a Builder stage, `readers`, between mine and starting_state, one proposal per requestor whose results the row extractor cannot read, cached on the results it saw, at most four attempts, each retry carrying the failures named per masked shape. Where a corpus has no such results (the retail and airline corpora) the stage is a no-op. (3) The proposal returns readers as code: per tool a sandboxed `read(result) -> dict | None` mapping a result string to column values, run by the gate over every recorded result of its tool. The gate is the free one: every reader parses, runs on every recorded result without raising, and answers a dict or None; the share of shapes a reader answers nothing for is reported per tool, not refused. Replay fidelity is the judge of the rest. (4) A body of such a tool gets the table in its schema block like any table and assembles its answer from the columns (D168); the readers' source is in the compile_tools stage version.

The declared alternative was built and measured against it: per column `stored` or `derived` with the derivation as code, per shape "acknowledgement", and a gate walking every Trace to hold all three to the recording. On the corpus the declarations never fired (all 28 columns came back stored, no tool only acknowledges), so its whole effect was one rule the free gate also carries, one extra attempt and half a cent. Founder: "okay free gate it is." Numbers, one build each through replay_reference: Tasks confirmed 0 and 0 of 183; writes matched 658 of 2,577 (25.5 percent) before, 1,540 (59.8) declared, 1,630 (63.3) free; the requestor's own calls matched 37.7 percent before, 50.2 declared, 56.9 free; the MMS capability check 1.5 to 81.7 percent in both, the status bar 1.2 to 46.4 and 74.4; $0.74 and $0.70. Two faults the experiment found in its own mechanism, fixed in the same change and remeasured in the report's section 8: the requestor's own writes never closed a column in the walk that reads a recording's starting row, so a value read after the user's own toggle stood as the start and 183 Tasks split into 393 as worlds that disagreed; the changed columns of a requestor's write are now mined the way `observed_effects` credits an assistant write (the columns whose parsed value differs across the call, in at least one recording) and close at that tool's calls. And a column no recording reads before its first write fell to the Environment's defaults (about half the row); it now takes the corpus's most common pre-write value, recorded as an assumption with the count in the stage ruling.

Why: the readers are the same mechanism the harness already trusts for bodies, code the model writes and the recording rules on, pointed the other way; a reader either parses a result no recording made or fails loudly, where a shape table would be silent; and the gate over declarations checked what the recording could not contradict. Cost: a Task whose recordings genuinely started in different device states is two Tasks (D74), and the frozen denominator carries the comparison; the status bar, which 12 tools restate, is assembled by every body from the columns, so one wrong starting column costs every call that restates it, which is the number to watch after the remeasure.

### D178. The floor cuts a protected result that is alone over the line, the harness takes the model's window, and the Examiner searches the records (2026-09-07)

Founder, reading the token stats: "it should compact set the token limit to be around 30-40% of the context length." The line was already 40 percent (D124) and the floor ran at the end of every turn; on retail build 14 it freed nothing. Turn 2 of the Examiner read five whole files and the context hit 466 percent; the guards protect the current turn and the tool output of the last two turns (D131), so the five reads were untouchable, the note said "still over the line: the rest is protected", turn 3 filed 24 findings on a prompt of 1,793,682 tokens, and the reads left the protected zone at turn 5. Over the round the Examiner wrote 3.9M tokens fresh against 153k read from cache, compacted 15 times (22 on the b12 model arm), and a beat that had compacted twice read what it had read before. Beside it, every harness was built on `ContextConfig()`'s default window of 200,000 while the model's is 400,000: no caller passed `window_for`. And the Examiner had no way to look across records but reading files by id, which is what the reads were for.

Decided, three parts. (1) After dropping the unguarded units, a floor still over the line cuts any guarded tool result whose own tokens exceed the line's: the entry's content on the active path becomes its first lines, the mechanical summary and a note naming the id, the size and the `recall` that reads the whole back; the entry keeps its id and place so the call stays paired with its result, the session file keeps every byte, and a compaction that only cut adds no summary message because nothing was forgotten. `ContextStats.cuts` and `tokens_cut`, and `floor_cuts` per agent in each round's counts. (2) The Builder's and the Examiner's harnesses pass `ContextConfig(window=budget.window_for(model.name))`; the agent core still may not import the budget, so the caller passes it; an unknown model keeps the default. (3) An eighth Examiner tool, `search(text, regex, kinds, tool, task_id, limit)` over Traces, Runs, Intents, status rows and Verifiers: one line per match (kind, id, Task, where it matched, 200 characters around the match), a count per kind first, the same `READ_CHARS` clamp and the same refused paths as `read`. The prompt shows one call and one finding grounded across Tasks from the counts. Measured read-only on build 14's workdir: a two-word phrase over every kind answers in 5.4 seconds over 564 MB of Runs, 0.0 seconds over the Intents alone. The Builder gets no search: its `status` and zoom read rulings it owns and it has no record surface, so a search there would be a new surface, not the same shape.

Why: the Examiner's beat is the round's judgement and the floor exists so that the beat is never made over a context the model cannot hold; the D131 guard was written for results the model still needs this turn, and a result larger than the line is one it cannot use whole anyway. Cost: a cut result is read back through `recall`, one call per cut, which is the number the stats now count. What to read on the next builds: the Examiner's fill and compaction count per round, expected under one compaction a round after D175 and this.

### D179. A row whose identity is more than one column is keyed by the columns the call was asked for (2026-09-07)

Founder: "Composite row keys ... dumb it down please", then "fix these please using sub agents." The airline-domain world held 122 flight rows keyed by flight number with `date: null`, mined from 226 distinct (flight number, date) pairs, and 19 traces showed one flight number on two dates: the last row seen won, a search answered an empty list for a date the world had lost, a status lookup answered one status for every date, and a reservation write landed on the wrong row. Fidelity sat at 64 of 119 for four rounds.

Decided, the miner's rule (`composite_keys` in `mine.py`): inside one trace, with no write between them, a table's rows repeat an id and their hard values differ, so one id stands for several rows. The column that joins the key is one of the table's own hard columns that is also an argument of the call that returned the rows, with a value that differs between the two calls; the key is the intersection over every conflicting pair of the corpus, so one odd pair cannot add a column and a table no argument explains keeps its single key. Four refusals, each with a test: a change on or after a write (D74's after_write rule), a soft column (D73), a column no argument names, a table with one version per id. Row ids stay strings, the key columns' values joined by a separator the schema names (`EntitySchema.composite_keys`, `EntitySchema.key_separator`, both defaulted, so the runner's records grew and the runner is re-frozen before the next build), the tables block shows the body writer the key's form, `match_table` in `gates/tool_runs.py` keys a row by it and reads a missing key part from the call's arguments. A partial sighting (a row seen with only part of its key) is folded into every keyed row whose known columns match, filling only what those rows were never shown holding, and is kept under its partial key when nothing matches (D41: no invented part). `synth.grow` (D107) still keys by the id column alone and cannot grow a keyed table; noted, not fixed.

Measured, one round over the same corpus (`docs/builds/composite-keys-2026-09-07.md`): one table of three got a key; rows 122 to 187 (160 observed, 27 synthetic pairs a status call named and no search returned); the search tool 275 to 287 of 291 recorded calls matched, the status tool 0 to 51 of 112; reads 1,128 to 1,191 of 1,349. Fidelity 63 of 119 against 56 the round before and the corpus's best of 64; References 38 to 62; trusted 5 to 19. Two costs of the change itself: 15 of 59 recorded flight writes now refuse not_found because their references sit in a nested argument list `referenced_ids` does not read (next), and 4 of 291 searches answer a synthetic row where the recording answered nothing. The status per date is still not in the world because that tool answers a bare string the miner attributes to no column: the key gives a row its identity, not its value. Cost 0.37 USD, 509 seconds.

Why: the key is read off the corpus, from what the customer's own tool was asked for, and not off a column name, so a room by night or a price list by version gets the same treatment; and the world's shape changes once, at the miner, where every body reads it.

### D180. A row is homed by the id the call asked for, revealed columns take the D73 classes, and only hard columns split a Task (2026-09-07)

Founder: "learn from all of the builds and improve the harness as much as possible." On the third corpus the assistant's lookup by id was called 1,057 times and replayed none: its rows carry several id columns and a noun ("detail") that names no table, so `_table_of` gave them no home and `extract_rows` dropped them; the body read a table that held five columns another tool had revealed and raised on every call. Beside it, the readers stage (D176) had turned 183 Tasks into 371, because every revealed column was hard and `trace_worlds` hashed every column, so a speed reading that never repeats split the Tasks that took one.

Decided, three rules. (1) Homing by the asked-for id (`mine._home_of`): a row a call answered is homed in the entity whose id column holds a value the call passed; where several of the row's ids were passed, the column named like the argument wins, else the first in the row's order; an id the tool's name marks as the address (the entities after its first preposition) is ignored, so a call listing a parent's children still homes the children; a column without an id suffix is ignored. The noun, distinct-across-siblings and only-id rules stay as fallbacks in that order; the decision per tool and the rows not homed go to `row_homes.json`. `EntitySchema.homes` keeps its meaning (nested rows) and the frozen record is untouched. (2) Revealed columns take the same D73 classes as mined ones (they already ran through `propose_column_class`; what did not hold was the hashing), plus one class rule for any corpus: a numeric column whose sightings never repeat a value (distinct equal to sampled, at least `MIN_UNIQUE_VALUES` of them) is a reading taken, not a fact stored, exempt at low confidence. `trace_worlds` and `readers.merge_worlds` hash hard columns only, which is what `split_by_world` consults. (3) An early `--target` stops before the Examiner with the inputs it did not build named (`examiner.stage.missing_inputs`), instead of `derive failed: KeyError`. One more: `id_pattern` refuses its wide fallback when an ordinary word matches it, because homing more rows gave one column that pattern and `canon._as_id` upper-cased every string in the world through it.

Measured, one round on a copy of the third corpus (`docs/builds/readers-experiment-2026-09-07.md` section 9): the lookup 0 of 1,153 to 914 matched; assistant side 45.7 to 79.2 percent of calls; everything 51.6 to 64.6 percent; Tasks at replay fidelity 0 to 8; cost 0.70 USD, 8m 53s. The 239 lookup calls that still differ ask for ids the Starting state does not hold (D40, the nested-argument fill is on the next branch). The Task count did not fall: 373. The reading that never repeats had split 20 Tasks and now splits none; the remaining splits are on the device's switches (signal, airplane mode, mobile data), where two recordings of one frozen Task did start in different device states. That is D74 doing what it says, and whether the customer's Task should be one Task with two starting worlds is the open question section 6 of the report raised.

Why: a lookup is the customer telling us which entity a row is, in the argument it passed, and that evidence outranks the tool's noun; a class is a property of the column's values, not of who revealed them. What to read on the next build: `row_homes.json` for rows still without a home, and the split count per revealed column.

### D182. A stated fact is required only when it was asked for, the judge sees what each state told the user, and list order is not an End state (2026-09-07)

Founder, on the failure patterns: "the verifier derivation, context for reference judge (we need agentic judge maybe) ... start fixing these patterns", then "yes read b numbers first." The derivation had turned every token a recorded answer stated that also sat in a tool result into a required communicate atom (about 70 percent of the exported assertions on the first two corpora carried a number this way), the judge saw End states only and failed recordings the corpus rewarded, and two recordings that wrote the same items in another order were two End states.

Decided. (A) A communicate fact is required when its token, or the field it was read from, is in the Intent's grounded phrases, the Intent line or a user turn of the Run; otherwise it keeps its value, span and predicate under payload kind `communicate_reported` and atom kind `allowed`, evaluated and reported, never rejecting (`verdict.py` is frozen and enforces any `communicate` payload, hence the second kind). A Verifier that would otherwise ask nothing demands its reported facts again, because a read-only Task with a vague request had ended with nothing any Run could fail: without that line the eight code checks passed 74 of 101 Tasks against 79 before. (B) Each group in the judge prompt carries the values its answers stated back and how many of its Runs handed the conversation on, with the transcript still withheld; and no cap of 0 writes is written when the Reference group wrote nothing while another confirmed Run of the Task did and the request asked for nothing else (the D133 held-out route, not a widened cap, since a wider cap would still reject the writing Run through `extra_writes`). (C) `settled_state()` orders every list argument and `group()` joins two states the settled form cannot tell apart, only when the tool answered both calls alike. (E) Every constraint with code gets a rate against the confirmed recordings or a reason, `judged` beside `failed` and `runs`, and `gates: false` on a rule that gates nothing; what a Verdict may check is unchanged.

Measured offline over the existing Runs (copies of the first two corpora's workdirs, 0.34 USD of judge calls): false rejection over the D133 pool 0.345 to 0.274 on the first corpus (14 to 11 Tasks at 1.0), 0.753 to 0.653 on the second (36 to 26); required stated facts 459 to 310 and 382 to 172; the eight code checks pass the same Tasks before and after (79 of 101, 20 of 62). The judge change kept 181 References against 175 and left 33 Tasks without one against 36, but its agreement with the corpus's reward moved 79.4 to 79.6 percent and it now fails 22 rewarded recordings against 18: seeing the stated values did not close the judge's gap. Two premises did not hold and were not built. Literal ids: all 55 `second_path_passes` failures are single-Reference Tasks where the check never ran (`not_run`, routed to `reroll_then_derive`), and over 458 held-out Runs zero wrote the same tool to an entity no atom names; every write rejection is a Run that wrote nothing (85 of 124 on the first corpus, 43 of 43 on the second), used another tool, or made some of the writes. The four empty Verifiers are the corpus's own answer-key sidecar for one task it ships empty. Vocabulary is not wired before grounding, and would ground 0 of the 16 short Intents on the third corpus and misground 7 of them, so it stays unwired.

Why: an atom is a claim the customer's own record supports, and a fact nobody asked for is reported, not demanded. Two things this leaves open, both for the next entry: the judge still fails one rewarded recording in three of those it can be checked against, which is the case for a judge that can look at the policy and the rows the groups touched; and a held-out Run that wrote nothing on a Task that asks for a write is counted as legitimate by the D133 pool, so most of the "false" rejections are true ones and the trusted gate is failing Verifiers for catching them.

### D183. A prose result is rendered back out of the row and the round trip is the gate; a no-argument tool whose results never change is a constant of the world; ids below the top level of a call's arguments are referenced (2026-09-07)

Founder, on the failure patterns: "all of these are valid fixes ... start fixing these patterns without overfitting but just generally improving the whole system." Three patterns had no owner. On the third corpus the device tools append a line composed from other columns (airplane mode, signal, data, battery); the readers (D176) parsed it into columns and the bodies rendered "none" where the recording said a state word (airplane toggle 0 of 241 calls matched, data toggle 1 of 192). On the first corpus a catalog listing answered only the categories the world's rows held (0 of 19 calls, 15 categories only on the recorded side). And `referenced_ids` read top-level arguments only, so a write naming its targets inside a nested list got no D40 fill (D179's 15 of 59 refused writes).

Decided. (1) The readers proposal carries, per tool, a render as code, `def render(row) -> str`, the inverse of the reader; the gate holds both to every recorded result by round trip, `render(read(result))` equal to the result with the row completed as the trace had it at that point, failures named per masked shape and fed back for up to four attempts; a tool that only acknowledges renders a constant; a derived value the row does not hold is the render's business and the round trip proves it. The body of a read tool answers the render and the body of a write tool applies its effect and answers the render; the body skill says so in one paragraph and the render's source rides in the body note. This is D176's declared arm in its cheapest form: one function per tool, no per-column declarations, proven by round trip. (2) A tool with no arguments (or arguments that never vary) whose recorded results are identical across every call and are not rows of a table is a constant of the world: one `constants` row with one column per such tool (one row per value would make a body write a literal id, which D162 refuses), in the Starting state and in the tables block; the mine stage writes `world_constants.json`; the constant rides in `Column.evidence` since the schema record is frozen. (3) `argument_ids` walks lists and dicts at any depth for values under an id-named key, reading sibling key parts from the enclosing object; the shape-only half (a value matching an id pattern anywhere) was dropped because it invented rows on the fixture.

Measured, one round each on copies. Third corpus: the round trip held 1,192 of 2,670 results on the first attempt and 3,189 of 3,303 (96.5 percent) on the fourth, 14 shapes still failing, all on the reboot; calls matched over all tools 64.6 to 72.2 percent, the requestor's 27 tools 58.0 to 65.7, the assistant's 9 (no render) 79.2 to 86.6; the permission grant 0.5 to 78.2 percent, the network mode setter 7.2 to 74.0, the APN reset 30.5 to 85.1; six tools fell (the data toggle 38.6 to 20.3, the payment request check 74.7 to 36.3), which is the body writer's own variance between rounds; the lookup 79.3 to 94.5 percent because its body was rewritten. Tasks confirmed 3 before and after; 0.79 USD, 15m 05s. First corpus: one constant of 50 entries against 35 rows; the listing 0 of 19 to 19 of 19; calls matched 97.3 to 96.6 percent and Tasks confirmed 179 to 165, the whole loss in one write tool whose body was rewritten this round (83 of 139 to 39 of 139); 0.39 USD, 10m 04s. The nested rule adds 47 referenced ids on the second corpus (17 not in the world, D179's refused writes) and none on the other two.

Why: a reader without its inverse leaves the body to guess what the reader threw away, and the round trip is the one check that needs no declaration; a listing that never changes is data the customer's system holds, not a body's business to assemble. What this shows beside its numbers: a round that rewrites every body when the schema changes loses Tasks to the body writer's variance (179 to 165, 83 of 139 to 39 of 139 on one tool), and D174's rule (a recompile replaces a kept body only by beating it) does not yet cover a body rewritten because its inputs changed. Next entry.

### D184. The previous body competes on equal terms when a tool is recompiled, a tie keeps it, and the gates recorded are the released body's (2026-09-08)

D174 refused a recompile that scored below the body a tool already had, but only on a narrowed rerun, and only by reading the score off the tool's row. A full run, which is what the stage does whenever one of its inputs moves, rewrites every body from scratch. D183's world constants round did exactly that: 13 of 16 bodies were rewritten, 11 tied on score for the body writer's own variance between rounds, one took the listing tool from 0 of 17 recorded calls to 17 of 17, and one regressed the item write tool from 31 of 74 to 16 of 74 with nothing about its own inputs having moved. `gates.json` compounded it: it is read as the ruling on the module the stage released and `red_lights` turns every failing row in it into work, and it was recording the losing attempt's rulings, so the Builder was asked to repair failures the released body did not have.

Decided. Every tool that has a body in `bodies.json` has that body replayed by `compile_env.grade_body` under the world as it stands now and the gates as they stand now, no model call, ranked by `attempt_score` against the writer's best attempt; `kept_score >= score` keeps the previous body. The score on the tool's row is not read: it was measured under the world of the run that wrote it, and the two are comparable only under one world. A body that answered nothing is `could_not_run` and is no candidate whatever it scores, or the tie would hand the tool back to a body a schema change broke. `kept_bodies.json` records per tool the outcome, kept or beaten or could_not_run, with both scores; it sits beside the stage's other records and not on the tool's row, because the row is a declared input path of a narrowed rerun and a decision written there would move the key of the next identical request. The writer never sees the previous body, since a body in the prompt is a body copied, but what that body fails at joins the lesson as D181 rule 6 hints, grouped by shape with counts, the held out split filtered out. And the gates recorded are the released body's, not the losing attempt's.

Measured offline: a harness check reproduces the const workdir's own `tool_builds.json` score for 16 of 16 tools, and rules 14 ties, 1 beaten, 1 kept, 0 could_not_run; evidence calls matched 2508 of 2569 before, 2510 under the rewrite, 2525 under keeping by beating. The cost is one extra sandbox replay per tool with a body on every full run. One deviation: D183's 83 of 139 to 39 of 139 figure is not on disk for the const workdir, where that tool has 74 evidence calls after the D74, D81 and D164 filters and the outcomes file agrees with the replay at 16 of 74. The assumption to read on the next builds is the per tool kept and beaten counts in `kept_bodies.json`, expected mostly kept once the bodies are good.

### D185. The held-out pool holds only the Runs that reached the Reference; the judge with a bounded look is measured, costs References, and ships as an opt-in (2026-09-08)

The founder asked for a judge that looks before it rules ("we need agentic judge maybe"); the rule agreed was to read the one-shot judge's D182 numbers first. They said it fails recordings the corpus itself rewards, 22 of them on the first corpus, usually because a state wrote nothing where the policy forbade the write. Neither that rule nor the row is in front of it, so the agent was built: six read tools, intent, policy, rows, stated, constraints and answer, with every compiled rule counted before any is shown; a cap of six calls held by a `tool_call` hook, so a batched turn cannot spend a seventh; the one-shot judge as the fallback over the cap or on an unparseable judgement; `judge_calls` and `judge_fallback` on the reference record; and evidence checked against this judge's own six sources, not the one-shot judge's three.

On read-only copies, two samples each. First corpus, 205 Tasks and 85 judged: References 121 to 107 in both samples, Tasks passing the eight code checks 89 to 79 in both, corpus agreement 79.3 percent to 76.0 and 75.4, judge-failed recordings the corpus rewarded 18 before against 30 and 7 after, 43 of 85 judgements over the cap and ruled by the fallback, 6.48 calls per judged Task on average and 7 at most, 0.088 USD against 0.080, abstentions 0 everywhere. Second corpus, 119 Tasks and 34 judged: References 47 to 37 and 43, trusted by code 28 to 24 and 27, agreement 76.4 percent to 79.5 and 75.5, judge-failed rewarded 20 before against 10 and 17 after, 15 of 34 over the cap. Two samples of the same treatment disagree by more than the treatment moves, and the cap binds on nearly every judgement. The lost References have a cause on the record: told a pass is not its to award, the agent fails nothing, the groups stay split, and the Task ends as recordings disagreeing on the End state.

Decided (Fable, 2026-09-08). The agent judge ships off by default behind an explicit `--judge-agent`, and the one-shot judge with D182's stated values and hand-offs stays the default. Trusted Tasks are the founder's goal and the agent costs ten of them on the first corpus in both samples, so nothing that costs References ships while its own samples cannot agree; behind a flag the next iteration is measured rather than rebuilt.

The held-out pool rule is independent of the judge, measured within every run, and ships on: the D133 pool keeps only the Runs whose settled End state equals the Reference's, the rest left out with a reason on the status row. False rejection fell from a mean of 0.278 to 0.133 on the first corpus (Tasks at 1.0, 10 to 4) and 0.167 to 0.0 on the second (1 to 0), Tasks trusted by code unmoved at 89 and 28, 156 and 43 held-out Runs left out, 75 and 35 having written nothing. The frozen gate needed no patch: `loosening.discarded_runs` reads `failed_recordings` off the Examiner's own status rows, so the filter sits in the Examiner at D173's seam and the loosening gate still sees the whole pool. Those numbers come from an offline re-derivation without the loophole probe and compare only against each other. Next to read is the confirmation the judge cannot see, the dominant wrong failure on both judges (9 of 28 judge-failed Tasks after against 5 of 39 before, 5 of 7 after on the second), where the judge names allowed sources and rests on an absent one and D93's check misses it: a code question about what a ruling must cite, and beside it a cap sized to the Task's number of End states.

### D186. A failure by the judge cites a value the states differ on, checked in code, or the judgement abstains (2026-09-08)

The founder read D185's closing finding and said "cool fix these please" (2026-09-08). The finding was that the dominant wrong failure on both judges is a confirmation neither can see: 9 of 28 judge-failed Tasks on the first corpus and 5 of 7 on the second rested on one. The judge has no transcript (D93) and names its allowed sources in `evidence`, then rests on an absent one anyway, so the list it writes for itself never catches this. What was missing was a rule about what a failing ruling must point at.

A failure is now a claim about one named value. The judge is asked only where the End states disagree, so each failure names the state it fails, a key it differs on, the value it holds there, and what that should have been: another state by label, `intent`, or `policy:<n>` for a numbered line it was shown. `differing_keys` computes the keys from the settled End state's columns and D182's stated values, holding nothing counting as a value, and each state's prompt block opens with those keys and the value at each, so a failure quotes back a string the prompt printed. `read_failures` checks all four against that same material; one failure that does not pass drops the whole judgement, `abstain_reason` reads "a failure rested on nothing the states differ on: <key>" on the reference row, and the Task falls where an abstention already fell. Rounds count `judge_uncited` beside `judged`. Both judges share the rule; the agent's tools mark the differing entries and number the policy lines they show.

Ten tests cover it on invented domains. The first cut printed the keys without the values and was wrong: 11 of 34 judgements on the second corpus were dropped because the judge wrote "no writes" where the prompt rendered "(no value in this state)". Printing the value ended that.

Measured read only on copies, the first corpus limited to its first 40 judged Tasks of 205, the second whole. One-shot judge, first corpus, two samples: References 63 before against 64 and 62, Tasks passing the eight code checks 49 against 48 and 47, agreement 81.9 percent against 80.2 and 80.0, judge-failed recordings the corpus rewarded 15 against 9 and 13, uncited 1 in each. Second corpus, 34 judged: References 46 to 48, checks 29 to 27, agreement 77.8 to 75.0 percent, rewarded failures 21 to 10, uncited 0. The agent judge, one sample each: References 63 to 44 and 46 to 36, checks 49 to 35 and 29 to 23, rewarded failures 15 to 4 and 21 to 1. 0.64 USD over 1103 calls.

Three things disagree with the premise. Re-judged fresh, the confirmation-shaped reason is rare, 1 of 34 judgements on the second corpus and 2 of 85 on the first against the 5 of 7 last reported, so resampling the judge moves this more than the treatment does. Almost nothing is dropped by the check now; the judge itself fails far fewer states once it has to point at a value. And the agent judge buys its low false rejection with References, 19 and 10 of them, so it stays off. Next to read is `judge_uncited` per round, the recorded reasons, and whether Tasks lose References to a judge that will not commit.

### D187. The scoring path honours column classes, results read as columns, result literals flagged, keyed lists unordered (2026-09-08)

The founder asked to observe from the errors and fix things generally, without overfitting (2026-09-08). Four things were seen across two corpora. First and largest, the path scoring a replayed Trace against its Reference never consulted the schema's column classes: compare_call in runner/replay.py canonicalized both whole answers under one hardcoded class, first_difference did the same, and canon.compare, the one function that honours the classes, was called nowhere outside canon.py. A created-at column the schema marks exempt failed a write on 12 airline Tasks, and every semantic column everywhere was held to a hard bar. Second, a tool answering in one prose sentence had that sentence compared as one hard value, although the D176 readers parse it into classed columns; about 240 of the 314 failing telecom Tasks. Third, the memorised-values gate of D162 scanned a body's literals against world ids and recorded arguments but never against a value a recorded result carried. Fourth, a list result was paired positionally even where both sides carried row keys.

The rule is one comparison. Every returned row, and every reading a reader takes out of a prose result, is compared column by column: an exempt column is equal whatever the two hold, a hard one by canonical string with the leaf named, a semantic one through canon.compare, which takes the judge where one is given and otherwise reports without failing, as since D73. An unclassed column takes the rules' default. The Runner cannot import the gates, so it is handed a comparer with one method, the way it is handed the canon rules. Lists of rows keyed on both sides pair by key. Rule (d) of the memorised gate flags a literal a recorded result of the same tool carried, and only where it is a datum rather than a word, because the first cut refused a correct write tool for spelling out the status word its own recording answers.

Twelve tests on invented domains cover the three classes in a returned row and in a reader's reading, a reader that reads nothing, keyed and unkeyed lists, a result literal flagged and a vocabulary word kept.

Measured offline on copies by re-scoring replays.json, with a no-comparer run reproducing the recorded numbers exactly. Telecom goes from 8 of 322 Tasks confirmed to 17, with 721 calls across 12 tools now agreeing and 79 keyed list results. Airline goes from 75 of 119 to 78, with 12 book_reservation calls now agreeing and no keyed lists at all. The memorised gate goes from 6 failing tools to 8 on telecom and stays at 2 on airline. No model was called, so the spend is zero USD.

Three numbers disagree with the premise. The prose fix was expected to carry about 240 telecom Tasks and carried 9, because a Task fails on the first tool that still parts, and those are hard columns the Starting state seeds differently. The airline baseline is 75, not the 61 the report cited. And _row_pairs already paired by key; compare_call never reached it. Next builds should read the columns field on a failing check, which now names the class, and watch telecom's hard columns once the Starting state is seeded per Trace.

### D188. Nested rows and homed partial reads pinned per Task (2026-09-08)

The founder asked to observe from the errors and fix things generally, and that the fix not overfit (2026-09-08). Two fidelity readings named the Starting state as the second largest cause: 22 Tasks of 119 on one corpus, 14 a status the seed world answers with one constant and 8 a search over connecting legs that finds nothing, and 29 of 322 on another whose starting value began in a shape its own recording never read. `extract_rows` tested only a result's top level, so a nested row was never a sighting and a keyless result matched no table at all.

The pinner now reads a result as a tree. Every dict at any depth is tested against the schema and every list walked; a dict that matches is a row and is still walked, because a row that embeds rows is a sighting of both. Depth counts the rows a row sits inside and not the lists, so the elements of a list a result answers are its own rows. The walk carries its path, so a structure pointing back at itself ends, and stops at eight levels, counting each stop. A row some result states on its own is described by those sightings alone: what a row carries inside another is about the thing it sits in, and taking a leg inside a booking for the row it names rewrote 44 rows the corpus had stated plainly. A row only ever mentioned in part is completed from the rows the corpus did state, the way a row it only referenced already was (D40), and tagged assisted. A keyless result whose call named a row is homed on it by D180's own asked for rule (`mine.asked_for_id`, narrowed by `_address_of`); an object states its own columns, a bare value only through that tool's D176 reader, and with no reader nothing is pinned and the result is counted. A Task's overlay is then built column by column, each column taking the earliest of that Task's own sightings that carried it, so a nested row, a homed read and a column the corpus disagrees on all pin alike. Two Runs disagree per column, not per row, and `overlay_pins.json` carries the counts.

Sixteen tests on an invented domain of routes and meters cover each rule, the depth cap, a cycle and a scalar read with an invented reader.

Measured on copies, code only, no model calls, zero cost. First corpus: Tasks confirmed 70 of 119 before and 76 after, rows pinned 773 to 1607, columns 8552 to 13974, the connecting leg cell 8 to 3. Two numbers disagree with the premise. The second corpus moved not at all, 8 of 322 with an identical reason table: its 29 Tasks are the simulated user's own tools, taking no arguments and answering prose, so they are the readers' rows and never the pinner's. And the first corpus's status cell rose from 15 Tasks to 17: all 112 of its keyless results were homed and none read, that corpus proposing no reader for an assistant's own tool. A first cut without the states its own row rule scored 66, worse than changing nothing.

One thing had to be repaired to land this. Tagging a row synthetic writes the id onto the schema, and the schema is the mine stage's artifact that every stage below it reads. A build served the Starting state from cache never runs the tagging, so from the second build on the schema passed downstream was a different one and every stage under it missed its own cache; nothing had noticed because the only corpus in the tests had no synthetic rows and this rule gives it some. The tag now goes on the stage's own copy, which is what `schema.json` carries, and the ids travel to the stages that need them as the `synthetic_rows` artifact they were already released as (`build.with_synthetic_rows`).

Next builds: read `overlay_pins.json` for `unread_partial_results` and `nested_sightings_of_a_row_already_stated`, and the assumptions naming a row completed in part.

### D189. A lone Reference is re-rolled for a second path, bounded (2026-09-08)

The founder asked to observe from the errors and fix things generally, without overfitting (2026-09-08). Read by reason, the largest single not-run in the D79 suite was check 5, which asks whether a second Run reaches the Reference's End state by a different call path. It was not run on 39 of the 113 Tasks that replay faithfully and are still not trusted, 28 on the first corpus, 11 on the second, none on the third. Nothing was wrong with those Verifiers: the D111 rule had settled a single Reference and nothing else in the Task's evidence reached it, and a check with no input counts as not passed (D173). A Task could clear eight checks and fail the gate on an input nobody had bought.

The rule is that the derivation buys it. Where the suite would report check 5 not run for want of a second Run, the stage re-rolls that Task three Runs at a time, the count the re-roll stage already samples, up to three batches, and stops at the first batch in which a Run reaches the Reference's settled End state. Reaching is not redefined: it is the D111 grouping call asked of two Runs, so a second path is the same writes with the same answers (D182) arrived at by other reads or other wording. The Runs join the Task's Runs like any other re-roll and are written to the Examiner's re-roll file under the reason second_path with their batch number, so a re-run reads them back and buys nothing, and the D177 key covers them because a batch bought is a different derivation. A Task that spends the cap keeps its not_run and records the reason, counted per round as second_path_exhausted beside second_path_found, second_path_batches and second_path_runs. Cost is bounded per Task by the cap and per round by how many Tasks stand alone, and a batch that hits the run ceiling ends the search and says so.

The arguable half is that these Runs corroborate the Reference and never re-open it. They are bought after the rule has settled, for one purpose, so a Run landing on another End state is recorded in the words the false-rejection pool already uses (D133) rather than re-running the confirmation over the wider evidence. The search cannot unseat the Reference it supports, and equally cannot correct a wrong one.

Six tests on invented domains cover a batch that finds the path and stops, the cap holding with its reason on the row, the batches read back on a re-run, a Task that already has a second path buying nothing, no Runner leaving the check untouched, and a ceiling ending the search. Measured on a copy of a live workdir, 119 Tasks, one derivation each way. Of its 13 lone Tasks, check 5 passed on 1 before and 7 after, none failed, and the Tasks clearing the whole suite went 1 to 7. Corpus-wide the check went 40 passed and 15 not run to 48 and 9, for 30 batches and 90 Runs on those Tasks, 120 over the corpus, 0.2182 USD, no batch at the ceiling. Two things disagree with the premise. Confirmed References moved 55 to 57, and one lone Task confirmed in one pass and not the other with no batch involved, so the fail-only judge resamples enough to account for roughly two of the seven. And six of the thirteen still exhaust three batches, so a Reference standing alone is often the Task having one path rather than a Run nobody bought. Next to read is second_path_exhausted against second_path_batches per round, and whether a Task passing on a bought Run stays trusted.

### D190. A required atom's value is one a user said, and a read-only Task can be falsified (2026-09-08)

The funnel named two reasons that stop Tasks which pass fidelity and hold a confirmed Reference. Nine failed the leak check because an atom carried a value no user of the Task said while the Intent or the simulated user's rules spelled it out. Thirteen failed the empty, wrong, unsolved, loophole and mutation checks together because the Verifier held nothing a Run could fail. Reading the atoms moved the diagnosis. All six leaks on the second corpus sat on a D182 reported fact, which rejects no Run. On the first each was a required write value beside a fact demanded only because the mined Intent repeated its number. The thirteen held a cap of zero writes and compiled policy rules and nothing else, so an empty Run satisfied everything.

The derivation now writes a demand only in something a user said. A write value that would be required, that provenance calls system derived, and that no user of the Task's Runs said, becomes a shape over the same column instead of the literal: the value must be one the Starting state holds under that column inside the row this write acts on, and where the row holds no such column, that the Run wrote something there. A user stated value keeps its literal, since D42 already compared it canonically against a user turn. A fact is demanded on the strength of its own number only when a user said that number, while the Intent's words still name a field. A reported fact carries no provenance, because provenance evidences a demand and this atom makes none, and provenance is what the leak check reads. Where nothing can still be failed, one claim is attached: on the tables the Task's tools touch, the End state is the Starting state, asked about every call so a read-only Reference has something to judge. The status row counts the atoms relaxed and the claims added, and the cache format is bumped so Verifiers derive again. Twelve tests on an invented locker room cover the rules, one asserting that this module's reading of what a user said and the frozen leak check's agree.

Measured offline on copies, no spend. On the second corpus's forty eight confirmed Tasks, all nine checks passing goes from twenty eight to thirty one with none lost: leak and mutation each forty two to forty eight, empty, wrong, unsolved and loophole forty two to forty three, second path thirty seven to thirty six. On the first corpus's five named Tasks, two pass all nine, one gains mutation, one gains nothing.

Three things disagree with the premise. The funnel called the leak reason a required value, and on the second corpus none of the six was required. The leak left on the first corpus is a fact the request genuinely asked for, whose constant reaches the Candidate through the mined Intent, so that repair belongs to the Intent miner. And the new claim moves no check where the Reference made no call and told the user nothing, because only a stated fact or a question can fail an empty Run. Next to read is whether a shape atom ever lets a wrong write through.

### D191. One scorer for a narrowed repair, and the calls the Reference replay fails on are evidence (2026-09-08)

Founder: "observe from the errors and fix things generally, it should not overfit" (2026-09-08). The observation: repair_recompile reports a repair round after round while replay fidelity does not move. Airline is flat at 61 of 119 over four closed rounds with 32 recompiles on four tools, telecom at 8 of 322 with 121 recompiles in one round, and kept_bodies.json shows 13 of 13 and 36 of 36 tools kept, none beaten.

Read on copies of the two workdirs, three of the four premises were wrong. A narrowed recompile already runs the same function as the full pass, compile_tools narrowed to one tool, over the same evidence, gate order and attempt_score, and already compares against grade_body of the body the tool has.
