D217 named two differences that are never cosmetic and left a third state unnamed. A comparison returned a boolean, so a pair nobody had settled came back false for different and read as the same. Where a schema classes a column semantic and the caller hands the comparer no judge and no table, that is every pair of that column: the gate called the replay a match, the context a Task's own checks run in answered same, and the round counted the call agreed. On one workdir 387 of 13536 replayed calls agreed that way, one column of eighty carrying all of them, and the judge meant to settle them was never wired into either place a build makes the comparer. A share over an empty denominator returned one for the same reason, so a half of a ruling with no call of its kind read as perfect rather than as unmeasured.

The rule. A comparison answers one of three ways, equal, different, or unresolved, and unresolved is never agreement. A replay records it as its own route and the fidelity ruling counts it a failure naming the column. An atom whose check cannot be settled is unevaluable rather than held, and the verdict says how many ended there, so a Task neither passes nor is failed on a question nobody answered. A Task's own check may name what an open pair counts as, and otherwise the exception carries the column and both values. The judge is wired where a build makes the comparer, with an equivalence table that persists across rounds, memoised by pair, and two judges where a second model is configured. The stage key carries the verdict format, the judge identity and the table version, so a changed judge or a grown table recomputes rather than reads a stale answer. A rate with no denominator is not measured rather than one, and a ruling that measured neither half fails.

Eighteen tests on invented kiln, lamp and ferry domains: an unresolved column failing the gate, a judged pair passing with its route recorded, a table answer costing no call, a policy settling an open pair and the exception where none is named, an atom going unevaluable, and an empty half reading not measured.

Measured offline on copies of two workdirs, replay only, spend zero. The first fell from 53 Tasks confirmed of 406 to 36, none gained, its 387 forgiven calls becoming 417 recorded failures, with 2160 semantic pairs compared, 87 equal on the canonical form and 1656 different. The second did not move, 207 of 213, its schema classing no column semantic. Three readings cut against the premise. The fall is not lost fidelity but a withdrawn claim, and it returns only as the judge or the table answers, which is why the judge is wired here and not merely counted. The gate that compiles a tool body has no judge to hand, so a semantic column there is now a failure the model must write around, beyond what was asked. And the unmeasured half stays in the metrics rather than the failure lines, since two callers show the model one failure line and this would crowd out why a body was wrong.

Read the route on a verdict before its rate; a rate of one over no calls is now absent rather than perfect.

### D220. A Builder stage draws from the seed Runs, and the world keeps what only a held-out Run witnessed (2026-09-09)

The founder asked for the held-out share to mean something (2026-09-09). It did not. D81 holds a share of each Task's Runs back so a Reference is scored against something the Builder never saw, but nine stages read the raw Trace list or the Task's own run_ids and so read every Run. On the two workdirs, 74 of 456 Runs and 25 of 200 were held out; the body writer got 14 and 33 calls from them, every Intent prompt saw its Task's held-out members, the vocabulary came from all of them, and D191 put a replay failure back as evidence 4 and 17 times without asking whose Run it was. The seed set was also anything no Task claimed, so 2 held-out Runs of the first corpus were seeds outright.

The rule is one filter at one place. The scheduler, not the stage, decides what a stage receives: a Builder stage is handed ctx.evidence, the seed Runs and the Tasks with their held-out members removed, while the raw Trace list and the unfiltered run_ids are replaced in its inputs by a sentinel that raises AnchorLeak on any use, naming the stage. A stage that truly needs every Run says so in a field on itself and appears with its reason in ALL_RUNS_STAGES, which a test walks the whole graph against. The substitution is by artifact shape, not by name, so a new stage inherits the filter.

The world stays complete and the provenance does not. The Starting state records, per row and column, which Runs witnessed the value it holds. A value only held-out Runs witnessed keeps its row and its column, so replay still answers from it, but the body writer's view masks it while keeping the column, so a body reads the column rather than memorising the value; the memorised-values gate refuses such a literal with reason holdout_value; and replay marks each call that answered from one, counted per tool and Task as a finding. Forty two guarded columns over 8 rows on the first workdir and 166 over 20 on the second, 9 and 46 distinct values. Seventeen tests on an invented domain cover it, including the graph walk and a stage that reaches for what it was not given.

Measured on copies, code only. Held-out calls reaching the body writer fall from 14 and 33 to 0; Intent prompts with held-out members from 72 and 25 Tasks to 0; re-rolls likewise; vocabulary from 74 and 25 Runs to 0; readmission blocks 4 and 17 calls. Replay fidelity is unchanged, 203 of 209 Tasks and 438 of 456 Runs, 88 of 122 and 137 of 200, because the world lost nothing. Of the answers replay gives, 57 of 330 calls on 30 Tasks and 116 of 921 on 25 come from a held-out value.

Two disagreements. The readers stage cannot draw from evidence because it runs before the cluster stage that makes the Tasks the held-out set comes from, so it is declared. And the Simulated user's rules stay derived from every Run, because the Examiner's false-rejection pool needs held-out Runs' rules; each fact is marked with whether a held-out Run witnessed it, and the only Builder consumer filters. Next to read is answered_from_holdout per tool against fidelity, and whether masking costs a body anything.

### D221. An Environment is published as a package of the rebuilt world and its graders, and fetched back verified (2026-09-09)

The founder asked to put the Environments out today, with an organisation card and a README section. A build's workdir already held an Environment; nothing could hand one to anybody. The world, the Task list and the Verifiers sat beside the customer's export, the Runs, the agents' transcripts and every model cache in one directory with no line through it, so sharing an Environment meant sharing the recordings that made it.

The rule is that a package is written from an allow list, never a deny list. The export copies the compiled env package, the per Task Starting states, the records the Runner loads a toolkit from, the frozen Task list and every Verifier, and nothing else; an artifact a later build starts writing is out until someone puts it in. An exported Task drops the ids of the recordings it was clustered from and gains the text of its mined Intent, because that text is what a candidate is given while the file it lives in holds the verbatim turns. A manifest carries the numbers, the runner, gates and harness versions and a hash per file, so a fetch verifies what it downloaded. Publishing is one commit tagged by round, and a release needs replay fidelity at or above 0.90 over Tasks; below it, only a preview whose card opens saying so.

The leak scan is the check that the two halves stayed apart, and it took two readings to design. Read literally, a string the corpus says and env does not, it failed the first real export with 198 findings in 20 files. Most were the harness's own vocabulary, which a corpus produced by an agent harness also says, so the harness source now accounts for a string too. The rest were answer keys: a Task where the caller gives an address mid conversation has it in the Verifier and nowhere else, and forbidding that forbids a grader. So the scan splits what is left by length: prose is a recording that got in and stops the export, a shorter string is a value and is counted as an echo on the card. All three export with zero recorded strings, the first with 8 echoes over 7 files.

Tests are an invented nursery domain: the export writes the world and no recording, the scan fails a copied turn and passes an elicited value, the release form refuses below the bar, a fetch refuses a tampered package, and a fetched directory compiles under the Runner's own loader and its tools run. Measured offline, code only, spend zero, the first corpus at 95.1 percent of 205 Tasks with 122 trusted, the second at 72.3 of 119 with 38, the third at 9.3 of 183 with none. One number disagrees with the premise: the first corpus's round line says 203 of 209 while the manifest says 195 of 205, because the round counts every Task with a status row and the export counts the frozen list D96 fixed, so the manifest now names what each build added after its freeze. The package also cannot carry the Simulated user, whose facts are read off the recordings, so a Task whose answer only the user gives has a Verifier no candidate can satisfy. Read next whether typed facts (D210) can ship.

### D222. The check a judge needs is run by the harness before the model is asked, so a verdict never turns on whether a model calls a tool (2026-09-09)

The founder asked to observe from the errors and fix things generally, without overfitting (2026-09-08). D92 gave every judge read-only views of the Starting state, the End state and the Verifier's output, and made a verdict with no tool call a refusal. That is a claim about a model's manners, not about the question, and the semantic judge D219 wired into the comparer holds no tools at all: it is handed two values and asked whether they mean the same thing, so there is nothing there to call. On the contributor model all nine distinct semantic pairs came back refused, every one for want of a tool call, and a probe on invented values reproduced it. Before D219 that refusal read as agreement and the pairs passed silently; after D219 it reads as unresolved, which fails. The same shape was read at the Reference judge on other models under D185 and D193, thirteen abstentions of thirteen. Nothing under kullback/ai carried a tool_choice parameter either, although both the Responses API and chat completions take one.

The rule is that the check comes first and the model second. For every judge use the harness runs the reads that question needs, over the same material a tool of that name would answer with, and puts them in the prompt as a checks section naming the tool, its arguments and its result. Two kinds are prefilled: each source the use hands its judge, and every tool it holds whose arguments are all defaulted, since a view that needs nothing chosen is one the harness can open for it. Only a tool whose arguments have still to be chosen is left to the model, and where one is left the first turn asks the endpoint for tool_choice required; a provider that refuses the parameter is asked again without it and the refusal is recorded. The refusal in the judge stays for the one case it now means something, a question the harness could prefill no check for, which is a bug here and not a habit there. Beside D186, an agreeing verdict must now name the check it rests on; one that names none is a comparison nobody settled, D219's unresolved route. Rounds count judge_checks_prefilled, judge_extra_tool_calls, judge_refused_no_check, judge_tool_choice_forced and judge_tool_choice_rejected, and kullback judge-smoke asks one model two invented pairs and prints resolved or refused with the route. JUDGE_VERSION moved to 1, so a ruling stored under the old rule is recomputed.

Fourteen tests on invented columns: a prompt carrying its checks, a model answering with no tool call and taken at its word, an empty checks section refusing as a harness bug, an equal answer without a citation coming back unresolved, a tool whose argument only the model can choose forcing the first turn, a provider refusing the parameter and being counted, and the smoke command reading resolved and refused.

Measured live on the invented pairs, no workdir touched. On the contributor model the nine pairs went from nought resolved and nine refused to nine resolved and nine right, five equal and four different. The smoke pairs resolve on that model and on luna, two of two and right on both. Under a dollar of the two allowed, over twenty-two small calls, read off the call count rather than budget.json. Two readings cut against the premise. The model asked for no further tool on any pair once the checks were in front of it, so rule 2's forcing bought nothing here and is load bearing only where a judge holds a view whose argument has to be chosen. And the gain is a judge that answers, not one shown right about a corpus: the replay of the live workdirs was cut for time. Next to read is judge_refused_no_check per round, which should stay at nought, and the share of equal answers arriving uncited.

### D223. A Run's transcript claims are checked against its state, and partial completion is a code-only number beside trusted (2026-09-09)

Founder, 2026-09-08: observe from the errors and fix things generally, it should not overfit. The reading behind this one separates what a Run claims from what its state received. Kullback grades the End state and never the words (D46), so a false claim never earned a pass, but nothing said why a Run failed: a Candidate that answered "I have cancelled that for you" while calling nothing and one that wrote the wrong row were one failure count, and they ask for two different repairs. It also reports a partial score where the harness reports pass or fail, so a Task every Run half finishes and one no Run starts read alike.

The rule is one module, kullback/claims.py, writing claims.json each round. A claim is a sentence of the Candidate's own turns, not a question and carrying no future cue, that puts a word in the past or the present perfect whose stem is in the write vocabulary. That vocabulary is mined and never listed per corpus: the words of each write tool's own name, the verb each sentence of its description opens on, and eight general verbs any conversation uses, all compared as stems. A claim is matched to the Verifier's write atoms by the value it states first and the tool it names second; it counts written when a matching atom moved, unwritten when none did, and a write no sentence mentions counts writes_unclaimed. Partial completion is atoms confirmed over atoms carried, each scored by verifier_suite.check_run over a one-atom copy of the Verifier, so the per-atom answer is the rule the gates already score under. It never replaces or gates trusted, and the report legend says so. Four counters land on the round, the report carries a section and two lines per Task, D209's record carries the mean as a band, and the Examiner files a verbless finding per flagged Task. Twenty-two tests over an invented pottery workshop pin the mining, the promise that is not a claim, the refused write whose claim is unwritten, the value match and the flag.

Measured on copies of both live workdirs, code only, no spend. Retail: 690 Runs, 2238 claims, 475 written and 1763 unwritten, 5 writes unclaimed, 479 of 575 failing Runs claiming (83 per cent), partial mean 0.90, 38 Tasks flagged. Airline: 536 Runs, 689 claims, 161 written and 528 unwritten, 31 writes unclaimed, 151 of 380 failing Runs claiming (40 per cent), partial mean 0.91, 7 Tasks flagged. Over the held-out pools the flag reads, retail has 42 failing Runs of which 38 claimed and airline 11 of which 8.

Three disagreements. The count is per sentence and not per action: retail averages 3.2 claims a Run because a conversation restates what it did, so claims_unwritten is an upper bound. Most of it is not empty talk: 1401 of retail's 1763 unwritten claims sit on Runs that did write, which is a matching limit and not a lying Candidate. And D210's end kinds do not decide goal_satisfied by state: user_sim._goal_done reads the write tools the transcript shows called, so a call the world refused ends the conversation satisfied while this counts that Run claimed_unwritten (D67). That is what the flagged Tasks point at, left for its own decision; read next whether they shrink once the end protocol reads state. One unrelated defect, found and not fixed: findings.py's runs_disagree rule files a kind records.FindingKind does not carry, so every such filing raises.

### D224. Synthetic Tasks are walked over the mined tool-call graph, run in the rebuilt world and reported apart (2026-09-09)

The readings show a shortage of Tasks, not a fault in one. Every Task is derived from a recorded trace, so a corpus of two hundred recordings holds two hundred Tasks and no more, and what needs many Runs starves: on the first corpus of 1292 Runs, 139 of the 206 Tasks carrying a false rejection reading hold no held-out pool at all, and on the second, 78 of 85 hold a pool of one Run or none. That wants more paths through the world the recordings already paid for, not new recordings.

The rule. A tool-call dependency graph is mined from the Runs on disk: a node is a tool, an edge from A to B carries one argument of B and one result path of A whose values matched inside a Run, the same value match row identity is read with, and a second kind joins a read to a write that touched a row the read touched. An edge weighs the Runs that showed it, never the calls, and no edge is invented, so a pair nothing chained can never be walked. A walk starts where a recorded Run started, follows edges by weight under a keyed draw, and stops when it holds the writes and distinct tools the requested band asks for; a band owing no more writes may not step into one. Arguments bind as the evidence weakens: the edge, then the nearest earlier answer, then one row of the rebuilt world all of whose values belong to each other, then the values the recordings showed. The walk runs through the Builder's own tool bodies, one world per walk; a walk a body refuses is discarded and counted, never repaired, because a refusal is the world saying the path does not exist. What survives is replayed so it lands on disk as a Run and goes through the derive path every Reference goes through, and its own second paths are its held-out pool. The Intent is written in symptom form from the askable values alone, a draft stating a record value being refused rather than edited. Nothing generated joins tasks.json: it lives under synthetic/ with its own index, its own report heading and its own names on the round line, so trusted still means a recorded trace replayed at fidelity.

Twenty five tests on an invented world of kites and spools cover the graph holding only the pairs the recordings chained, an edge weighing Runs and not calls, a walk holding its band, one seed drawing one walk twice, a refused write discarding and counting, the executed End state being what the Verifier came from, and nothing synthetic reaching tasks.json.

Measured on copies. First corpus, code only, spend zero: 16 nodes, 203 edges, 179 by value and 24 by row, six starting tools, weights 1 to 854, median 12. Four bands asked twenty each; of 162 walks, 25 refused, none crashed, 57 bound no argument, leaving 80 Tasks, 49 passing the D79 suite, 78 with a measurable pool. By band: no write, twenty, none passing, mean pool 2.0; one write, twenty, all passing, 2.0; two writes, twenty, fifteen passing, 3.6; three writes, eighteen in band, fourteen passing, 3.9. Second corpus: 13 nodes, 221 edges over 785 Runs, the Intent writer live at 0.0018 USD.

Three things cut against the premise. The shortage named is the pool, and generation does not cure it: a synthetic Task's pool is 2.0 to 3.9 Runs wide, as thin as the recorded ones, so what is bought is Task count, not pool depth. The graph is not the limit either: 57 of 162 walks ended on an argument nothing in the rebuilt world could bind, against 25 the world refused, so reachable rows and not mined edges decide the yield. And the read-only band is worthless as built, twenty Tasks and none passing, because a walk that writes nothing leaves the Starting state and the derive path has no claim to make; that band should be refused rather than filled. Read the refusals per corpus on the next build: they say which precondition the graph is missing, and a corpus whose refusals stay high wants an edge mined, not a walk retried.
### D225. Task archetypes are read off the domain's own public material, and walks are shaped to them (2026-09-09)

D224's walks of the mined tool graph answered the shortage of Tasks and its own numbers said what that bought. Of 162 walks on the first corpus, 25 were refused by a body and 57 bound no argument, 80 became Tasks and 49 passed the suite, and every one was a sequence some recorded agent happened to chain. The read-only band produced twenty Tasks and no passes, because a walk that writes nothing leaves the Starting state and there is nothing to derive a claim about. Nothing in the graph knows what a person in this domain writes in about.

The rule. The harness reads a domain's own public material as evidence about its customers. Sources are URLs the caller hands over, a search run only where a key is set in the environment whose name is stated and whose value is never printed, and pages a model names for a one-line description of the business, recorded separately from what the caller supplied. A source under the corpus's own repository or paper is refused and counted whoever proposed it, so a benchmark cannot read its own published task list back in. Fetched text lives only in a content-addressed cache under the workdir. A reader extracts archetypes as structured records and never as prose: the goal in the customer's own first person, the situation, the effects in the words of the world, whatever rule the page states, and the source with a byte range. A record written from the operator's side is dropped and counted, since an instruction to staff is not evidence about customers; so is one that repeats a sentence of its page, or shares six consecutive words with a corpus Intent, which is contamination and not a leak. Two goals are one semantic pair and are settled the way the harness settles every other one (D219), and a pair nobody settled never folds. The plausibility judges take the archetype's own lines as their prefilled checks, and a question with no line behind it is refused rather than put to a model. Each surviving archetype is mapped onto the graph by a model that sees tool names, argument names and result columns; code keeps only tools that exist and writes some recorded start can reach, and the rest become coverage gaps, which go on the package manifest and the published card so a package can be judged before it is downloaded. A shaped walk must visit the mapped write tools, still following recorded edges and binding to reachable rows, and every Task climbs five rungs in order: attested, executable, graded, uncontaminated, plausible. Two judges may each reject a Task by citing the archetype line it contradicts and neither can ever pass one. Nothing shaped enters trusted, fidelity or a confirmed Reference.

Measured on reduced copies of two workdirs, two public pages per corpus that name no customer, with the corpus's own repository and paper excluded. Both graphs were mined first: 16 nodes and 182 edges over 609 Runs, 13 nodes and 151 edges over 258 Runs. The first corpus read 21 archetypes, none contaminated, none copying a page, none from the operator's side, three folded, 14 mapped and 4 gaps; the second read 18, none dropped by any scan, none folded, 6 mapped and 12 gaps. Shaping asked for 28 and 24 Tasks and kept 6 and 9. Of what fell, 78 and 51 fell at executable, 58 and 68 at plausible, 0 and 5 at attested, and nothing at graded or uncontaminated. Twenty plain walks per corpus gave 20 and 20 through the suite from 32 walks each. The reading cost 0.045 and 0.027 USD and the shaping 0.035 and 0.051.

Four readings cut against the premise. The reading is not the bottleneck and neither is the graph: the plausibility judges removed 126 of the 178 shaped attempts that ran, more than every other rung together, so what limits a realistic Task is whether the walk the world allows is the walk the person asked for. The two contamination scans never fired on either corpus. The second corpus put two thirds of its archetypes in the gaps, which says the rebuilt tools cover much less of that domain than the recordings suggest: compensation for a cancelled or delayed journey, care during a long wait, group handling and paper documents, none of which any rebuilt tool touches. And the model naming pages from a one-line description proposed eight customer help centres per domain where a hand-written list held encyclopedia and payment documentation, of which two and four resolved. Read the gap list first on the next build.

The fetch checks its destination before the socket (added 2026-09-10 on a review finding that it opened whatever URL it was given, and the URLs are not the harness's own). One pure function of the URL and an injected resolver refuses any scheme but http and https, an empty host, loopback names, literal addresses of any kind, and a name any of whose resolved addresses is loopback, private or unique local, link local, multicast, reserved or otherwise not publicly routable, with the classes read off the standard library's address types rather than a hand-kept list of ranges. A name resolving to a public and a loopback address together is refused. Redirects are checked on every hop, capped at five. A refusal carries the scheme or the class in words and never an address, and is recorded as a refused row of the crawl or a dropped source of the naming. Eighteen cases across eight tests cover it and none opens a socket. Not taken: resolving the name once and connecting to the approved address, which would replace the connection layer under urllib and ship untested on the one path a live reading depends on; the gap is written into the function's docstring.

### D227. A write counts as made only when its result shows it took effect (2026-09-09)

The founder's ask stands: observe from the errors and fix things generally, it should not overfit. Two errors were read off the D223 builder's report. The Simulated user decided its goal was done from the write kind tools the transcript showed called, so a write the world refused ended the conversation goal_satisfied while the same Run was graded as claiming a write it never made, and that graded fail landed in the held out pool that decides whether a Verifier is over strict. The second error is smaller and total: findings were filed under the kind runs_disagree, which FindingKind did not carry, so every such filing raised on validation and the D213 finding never reached the Builder.

The rule is that a write counts as made only when its own result shows it took effect. The Runner records a tool result as a result plus, where the world refused or failed the call, an error carrying that refusal's class in the D67 taxonomy, and that marker is the whole of the reading, because it is the one field the Runner writes on every route it can refuse on whatever payload the tool answered with. Where a write effect record rides with the call the record decides and has to show a column moved. A call requested whose result never came back is not a write that happened, so a name seen only in an assistant turn's tool calls no longer counts. The two Simulated users read this through one function in kullback/user/rules.py, which the shim re exports and which the agent user's end protocol now calls instead of its own copy, so the rule user and the agent user cannot disagree about whether the goal is done. A refused write leaves the goal open, the user restates the goal once and the Run reaches the same end kinds as before. FindingKind gains runs_disagree and the verb stays none. The round gains user_ended_on_refused_write.

Two frozen files moved and both are named here. kullback/runner/records.py gains the kind. kullback/runner/loop.py puts the refusal marker on the tool message the Simulated user is handed, because the user is given the transcript and nothing else, and a refusal it cannot see is one it cannot act on; the wire shape in kullback/ai/provider.py became a whitelist of the keys a chat message carries, so no mark the harness writes beside a message can reach a provider.

Twenty three tests on invented library, garden and delivery domains cover a refused write leaving the goal open and the restatement, a write that took effect satisfying it, the refused Run still reaching an end kind, an effect record holding no row, a mined effect row whose column holds what it held, a mined row whose column moved, the replay's own check record on both sides of a failure, a requested write with no result, both users agreeing on the same transcript, the round count, the finding validating and filing, and the wire dropping the marks, with the round count reading the end kind alone, in both the shapes the two users write it in, so a Run the Candidate closed over a refusal is not read as a goal the user said was done and a Run whose kind is a tag is not read as a Run that ended in no kind at all.

Measured on copies of two workdirs, code only, spend zero. On the first, 1318 stored Runs, 900 of which made a write, and none of them ended goal_satisfied over a write that was wholly refused. On the second, 813 Runs, 208 made a write, and none ended that way either. The first reading of these two workdirs said 6 and 14, and the review found why: it read the stop reason, which is user_stop whichever way the user ended, as a goal the user closed. The count now reads the end kind alone and names the Runs that carry none, which on both workdirs is every Run that wrote, all 900 and all 208, because both predate the kinds. So this decision has no stored Run to point at yet and says so rather than claiming twenty. Three numbers cut against the premise. The premise says 38 of 42 failing held out Runs carried an unwritten claim; the held out pool is not addressable from task_status.json on either workdir, whose rows carry did_not_reach_reference and failed_recordings and no pool, so that count could not be reproduced and what is measured is 6 and 14 Runs over the whole store. Neither workdir's overlay_pins.json carries a runs_disagree key at all, both predating D213 and D216, so zero findings would have been filed today and the ValidationError this fixes has never actually fired on a stored round. And the effect half of the rule was written against no record at all, since this branch was built before D215 landed. On the merged tree it reads D215's own records: the rows the Builder mined off the recording for that call, each naming a table, a row, a column path and what that column held before and after the write, and the replay's own count of the effect checks it ran with the columns that never reached their after value. Neither record rides with a routed call yet, because the route result the Runner returns carries no effect field, so the reading is exercised by the tests and by any route that comes to attach one rather than by a stored round.

Read user_ended_on_refused_write on the next builds beside the claimed_unwritten count, and read whether any runs_disagree finding reaches the Builder once a round is mined under D216.

### D229. DeepSeek and OpenRouter are reachable by name, through the registry, not through adapters of their own (2026-09-10)

Founder, 2026-09-10: make DeepSeek and OpenRouter first-class model providers, the general way, and CheaperInference beside them. The general way already existed: D116 put the models.dev snapshot on disk, and model_for resolves an unknown provider from the same file, so a provider is a row and not a class.

The rule, five changes, each a table or a one-line rule rather than a branch. A local provider registry (built-in rows in pricing.py plus an optional providers.local.json beside the snapshot) is laid over the catalog inside refresh, so a provider models.dev does not list, and a model row it does not carry, come out of the one lookup that already answers the host, the key variable, the price and the window. An overlay row is merged key by key and only where the shape underneath matches, so a file correcting one rate writes that rate and leaves the rates beside it, the window and the name standing; a rate given as a string leaves the number underneath alone, a key with nothing underneath is taken as written, and a rate or window that is not a finite number reads as absent on both sides, since NaN is a number to float() and to nothing else and billing one makes every total it touches NaN. A row whose models field names nothing readable is left out whole. The request-shape table gains @openrouter/ai-sdk-provider, because that package differs from the compatible one only above the wire and it is the wire the table is about. An endpoint reached by base_url takes its key from PROVIDER_API_KEY instead of inheriting OPENAI_API_KEY, which had been offering one vendor's key to another's gateway. The wire whitelist gains the reasoning fields, because DeepSeek and OpenRouter both document that reasoning must be passed back on a request carrying tools or the API answers 400. The login menu derives its range from its own length and puts every choice through the refusal /login itself would give it, because a choice that fails on the line after picking it is worse than one that was never listed.

The fifth is what the builds were blocked on, and it is about names. An id is only a model together with its provider. budget.priced_model_id keyed a call's price on reply.model and dropped the provider whenever that name already carried a slash, which is the shape a gateway answers in: asked for a flash model, one answered z-ai/ that model, thirty resellers carry that bare name at thirty rates, the disagreement rule that exists to stop a five times mispricing refused to price it, and the budget gate failed the Run at every call unpriced. Every OpenRouter model would have gone the same way. budget.py is frozen, so the adapter names its reply by the id it was asked under, provider included, and the endpoint's own name stays in raw and on the Exchange. A provider's row is found by its own key or by the one whose last segment matches, and only when exactly one does.

Live rates. A gateway's own listing served input at 0.105 where the row copied from it hours earlier said 0.060426, so the ledger was under-billing by nearly a factor of two on every call. A registry row may name its own price list, a url and the field each listed model carries its rates under, and refresh lays those rates over the row's models under the same live switch every other call is under, and only while a key for that provider is held. The written rates become the fallback: an unreachable list, an error, a renamed field or a rate that does not read as a number leaves them standing, and a list can only reprice a model the row already offers, never add one. Each list is read once a process, because refresh runs wherever an id is resolved and asking on every lookup would be a request per Run for a number that moves in days.

Measured live on the two keys held. judge-smoke resolved two pairs of two, right on both, on deepseek/deepseek-flash in 4.2 seconds and on cheaperinference/glm-5.3-flash in 12.0 seconds. A two-turn tool round on DeepSeek: 1050 ms, 306 input and 73 output tokens, a well-formed call, reasoning_content 84 characters, prompt_tokens_details.cached_tokens present beside the two cache counters. Eleven tests on an invented gateway and a stubbed listing cover the live rates winning, each of the four fallbacks, a listing that spells one model two ways, the key coming from the variable the row names, the call not happening while live is off or no key is held, and the list adding no model.

Three gaps. AssistantMessage forbids an extra field, so the loop drops reasoning_content before the whitelist can pass it back; the transcript type is where it would be fixed. budget.price_source reports an overlay row as models.dev, because the overlay sits inside the one catalog lookup and budget.py is frozen. And the snapshot's deepseek-v4-flash prices, 0.14 and 0.28, disagree with the vendor's published 0.30 and 1.20 for the model that id now routes to, so the snapshot is stale rather than wrong about the shape; read whether a fresh snapshot carries deepseek-flash, and drop the overlay row when it does.

### D230. A tool asked about a Task it cannot serve answers with the Task's state, and only a session with no output at all stalls a round (2026-09-10)

One environment's round 1 ended on the exit stalled after about forty five minutes of spend, because a derive raised a LookupError over a Task with no Reference on disk, and every finding that beat had queued went with it. The Task's row named a Reference made of one re-roll that no replay row and no re-roll row of the session carried, so the join from row to file came back empty and raised, out of the harness's own loosening step and out of the derive tool, and the driver read that error result as a broken agent.

Two things were wrong and both are general. The first is that a tool asked about a Task in a state it cannot serve answered with a traceback. A Task whose Reference cannot be read has nothing to derive a Verifier from and nothing to score a version against, which is a state of the Task and not a fault in the call, so the answer carries that state: how many of its Runs were turned down, the reasons with a count each, whether a Reference is named that this session cannot read or the Task never had one, and the verbs that buy something instead, which are reading its Runs, refusing the Task, and filing a finding. A repair gets the refusal as a result and not as an error, and the loosening step, which is the driver and not the model, writes it as a fourth reason for proposing nothing beside the three D218 rule 3 wrote.

The second is the boundary. A session that called derive and read an error back is not a broken contract: the model saw the error and could act on it, so the error is counted on the round's record and the round closes on what the beat left. Only a session with no output stalls: a model that never called derive, and the code driver's single call, where an error leaves nothing. A round whose derive errored and had no clean derive of its own may not exit done, since the counts an exit reads are then the round before's; it ends on the soft stop with the error named, and a finding still owed the Builder clears that exit and buys the beat the exception threw away. A beat that derived cleanly before the error did move its counts, so it closes on them.

Ten tests on invented cancellation and greenhouse domains cover the repair refusal, the two states of a missing Reference reading differently, the loosening step skipping the Task with its budget unspent, a derive error counted on the beat, the round refusing to close as done, a beat that derived before it errored closing on its own counts, and the queued finding still reaching the Builder. One thing cuts against the premise: the row was said to hold an empty references list, and the workdir shows a Reference named whose Run the session cannot reach, which is why the refusal tells the two apart.

On the next builds read tool_errors on each round record, and the auto_loosen rows for the new reason: a Task there every round is a Reference the derivation keeps withdrawing, a finding about the Reference and not about the Verifier.
### D233. Caller-side state is homed to a row like every other sighting (2026-09-10)

The state the caller's own device holds was filed per recording as one content hash of the whole recorded sentence, keyed by the answering tool and the requestor, never by table and row id. So nothing pinned it into a Task's overlay, rebuilt read tools answered a default instead of the recording's starting state, and two Runs whose sentences differed anywhere could not share a Task. On the third environment this is 1116 of 1208 differing checks in the round-2 pass, 116 of 135 failing Tasks, and all 43 frozen_only Tasks; the other two environments have the same shape on a smaller scale (82 and 21 checks).

The rule. A caller-side sighting that states columns of the table the schema mined for its requestor is homed to that table, one row per requestor, accumulated column by column with the same earliest-wins loop `trace_worlds` uses for a customer row. The split then compares it per column under the schema's column classes: each hard column is its own world key carrying its version hash and its class, and a semantic or exempt column is left out, so it no longer splits Tasks. (The brief calls that class cosmetic; the schema record calls it semantic.) A prose sighting keeps the opaque sentence key even where a table is mined, because no reader may read its columns in the split, as does a requestor the schema mines no table for, so nothing that used to split is silently merged. The cluster reason says which case applied: the row case names the column, its class, the table and the tool, and the fallback says the whole recorded sentence is the version. The homing hash carries the revealed tables' classes and nothing else new, and the grouping format is bumped, since the split may now depend on them. The bump is documentation until the cluster stage wiring reads it (see the todo line for its owner): it is safe because the homing hash moves for exactly the Environments whose split changes, both keyed off the same revealed_by columns, and the cluster stage's code version includes compile_env, so the split is recomputed regardless.

Nine tests on an invented apiary domain cover a requestor sighting becoming a row in the mined table with its columns, earliest-wins accumulation across two reads, the row pinning into the overlay with a compiled read body serving it, Runs differing only in exempt or semantic columns sharing a Task, a hard difference splitting with the reason naming the class, a write closing the requestor's world, prose keeping the sentence key, the no-table fallback with its reason, and the homing moving with a revealed reclassification while a customer-only schema stands still. No existing assertion was edited; the readers test that no revealed table reaches the split still passes on its prose fixture and now describes the fallback only.

Not measured live; the next launch of the third environment measures it. The committed offline script runs no readers stage at all: its help lists ingest, mine, cluster, canon rules and Starting state, and it calls no model. So no revealed table is mined under it and the D233 path is never exercised. Read Tasks confirmed before and after with frozen_only before and after on that launch.

Four readings cut against the premise. The brief names the classes hard, cosmetic and exempt; the schema record carries hard, semantic and exempt, so cosmetic is read as semantic throughout. Prose caller tools still split wholesale on any sentence difference, reader-free column projection being nonexistent, so whatever share of the 1116 checks is prose does not move with this. The change departs from D216 on purpose for the revealed tables: their classes now reach the split, while every customer table stays a function of the recordings alone. And the pinning half of the fix rides the readers stage as before: a caller-side row reaches the overlay through the revealed rows it already produced, and a corpus whose caller tools answer in prose without a proposed reader still pins nothing.

### D234. An ambiguous effect is credited to the write that owes it, not to the last call (2026-09-10)

The founder's ask stands: observe from the errors and fix things generally, it should not overfit. Where more than one write sits between two sightings of a row, every write was credited with the moved columns and only the last was checked, on the reasoning that the recording constrains the world only once all of them have run. In a corpus where a caller-side lookup is classed as a write, the last credited call is frequently that lookup, so a tool whose role is to report a state is failed for a transition it never performs, while the create write that actually owed the transition is credited and never checked.

The counts from the read-only report: all 51 effect misses on the third corpus sit on ambiguous columns, 42 of the 51 are charged to a lookup, 38 of those 42 sit on a call already failed for the same cause, and 410 of 411 observed effect columns there are ambiguous. On the second corpus 31 of 117 effect misses are ambiguous, on the first 0 of 102, so the fix is free on the first and worth about a quarter of the second corpus's effect failures.

The rule narrows the credit before narrowing the check. An ambiguous column is charged to a write only where that write's own arguments name the row the column sits on, or where the same tool has been seen to move that column alone elsewhere in the recordings this pass was given, which on the replay-evidence path is that Task's Runs. The prompts read the proof over the seed traces (build.py:762), and the evidence reads it per Task over that Task's Runs (build.py:1504), so the second arm may fire rarely on a short Run set. Where neither holds for any write in the span, the column is filed once, under the last write, as unattributed and unchecked, and rides the evidence under its own key rather than under any call, so the round can count what the recording left unowned instead of failing the call that came last. Both tests are over the recordings alone, and D215's rule stands: a write is still judged on every row it moved, and a single-write span is credited exactly as before. The writer's prompt no longer shows unattributed columns to any tool. The runner half is written as a frozen patch beside the branch: the check counts the flagged rows on the check instead of failing them, and the round carries their total. Until the re-freeze the builder side runs alone, which already stops the misattributed failures because no per-call evidence is emitted for such columns.

Ten tests on the invented lending domain: a create naming the row owns the column over a later lookup; a tool proven alone elsewhere keeps the credit; neither holding yields the unattributed key; a replay under the frozen check fails nothing on the last call; a forged id equal to the tag bumps the key and loses nothing; a span with two owners checks only the last and keeps the ambiguous count; a single-write span keeps its flags; unattributed columns reach no writer's prompt; the row-naming credit pins placement and flags in two tests and the two-owner ordering pins the checked order, the evidence and the counts in two tests, to hold the ceiling.

Not measured live; the next launches measure it. The scripts on hand need a workdir or a model, and the live workdirs are out of reach from this branch, so there is no read-only run to point at. The invented fixtures move from 5 columns over 2 calls with 4 ambiguous and 3 checked to 3 owned columns over 1 call with 1 checked and 2 ambiguous carried as unattributed, while the single-write, starting-pin and two-trace fixtures are byte identical apart from the new keys.

Three readings cut against the premise. The two old-rule tests are removed, since rewritten they would duplicate the new unattributed and replay tests; that coverage sits in test_a_column_no_write_owes_is_unattributed_and_not_charged_to_the_last_call and test_an_unattributed_column_fails_no_call_at_replay. The ambiguous flag itself does not move: which columns are marked ambiguous is the miner's job and is untouched. And the downstream mark is untouched with it, so a read that parts over an unattributed column keeps whatever blame the row-level rule already gave it, which a separate change owns.

Follow-up 2026-09-10. Review on the PR read the two failing tests as unmergeable, which is right: a suite with two failures cannot merge. Both pinned the old credit on the same fixture, where neither write names the row and no lone move exists elsewhere, so rewritten they would duplicate the new unattributed and replay tests. Both are removed; that coverage sits in test_a_column_no_write_owes_is_unattributed_and_not_charged_to_the_last_call and test_an_unattributed_column_fails_no_call_at_replay, and the builder chunk is green.

### D243. The model client's read timeout fits the call (2026-09-10)

One posting client fixed the HTTP read timeout at 60 s and nothing could change it. A code-generating call at the size the compile_tools stage makes measured 81 s and 145 s wall on 2026-09-10, while a one-word reply took under 5 s and the model list under 1 s, so the provider was healthy and the timeout was simply shorter than an honest answer. Each timed-out attempt retried five times with backoff, so one long answer cost about six minutes of nothing and then ended the round on RetryExhausted.

The read timeout is now a field of the posting client (`kullback/ai/provider.py`): 300 s by default, overridable per process through `KULLBACK_MODEL_TIMEOUT_S`, read once at construction, where a value that does not parse is an error naming the variable. The post carries a split timeout object, so establishing contact still fails fast at 10 s while reads and writes share the budget. The incident row carries the timeout in force (`Exchange.read_timeout_s`, beside the timing, attempts and request id from D159), and a `RetryExhausted` raised on a timeout names that budget, so the next such log line explains itself.

Five tests in `tests/ai/test_timeout.py` on an invented domain cover the default split, the override, the unparseable value, a timed-out first attempt that then answers with attempts 2 and the timeout in the incident fields, and five timeouts raising with the budget in the message. Left out: the default is not surfaced beside the model flags in the README, since no section there lists environment variables yet.

### D232. A build names the model that drives the Simulated user (2026-09-10)

The third environment's round 1 ended 91 of 135 Runs with the scripted user running out of turns (end kind scenario_exhausted) while the agent user drove 0 Tasks against 48 rule driven, because `kullback build` had no way to name a user model. D214 built the agent user as a package with the driver choice and the fallback already implemented, and `kullback/builder/build.py` already threads a `user_model` read from `models.get("user_agent")`; what was missing was the flag and the plumbing from the CLI and the rounds loop into that dict.

The rule is one flag. `--user-model <provider/model>` on `kullback build` resolves through the same live model path as `--judge-model` and lands in `models["user_agent"]`, so the agent user drives every Task it beats the rules on while the rules stay the floor and the fallback exactly as D214 built them. Without the flag nothing changes: the entry is None and the keys, the cache and the Run ids are the ones they were before. `--user-model` without `--model` is allowed, since the Builder may be code driven; without a Builder model there are no re-rolls, so the user model pays only for the offline driver scoring. The user model id is recorded in report_config.json beside the judge models, so the build's report config says which model drove the user; a rebuild that drops the flag clears a stale user model id, so a rule-driven build is never attributed to an earlier model, while a workdir that never carried the flag still has nothing written. No report names the user model yet; the reader is left for the report package's owner (docs/todo.md). The round counts already carried `tasks_agent_driven`, and no other command runs re-rolls with a user, so only `build` takes the flag.

Five tests on an invented garden centre domain: the flag reaches the driver with the named model id and defaults to None, the model lands wrapped under `user_agent` with the rules alone without it, the workdir record carries the user model id, a rebuild without the flag clears a stale user model id, and a stub user model that wins the driver choice is counted, so `tasks_agent_driven` reads 1 where the same round without it reads 0.

Not measured live; the next launch of the third environment passes it. No build was run here, so the 91 of 135 and the 0 against 48 are the brief's counts, unread on this branch.

No disagreements. One smaller reading was taken: the `run` command's Candidate batches keep their rule-driven user, since they are batches and not re-rolls.

### D245. The single message stages send a stable system head (2026-09-10)

The token readers found the intent stage and the one shot reference judge sending one user message per Task with no system message, so the provider prefix cache reads nothing (cache share 0.00 on every arm) while both stages still pay a cache write premium (on one arm intent wrote 904707 tokens to read back 4550). The invariant instruction lines are the same bytes for every Task; only the evidence differs.

Decided: the intent call sends the invariant lines (the three instruction lines and the closing shape line) as the system message and the run sample, the repair hint and the attempt feedback as the user message; the one shot judge sends the fixed prologue as system and the per Task case (intent, policy lines, end states, and the second pass stop rule where it applies) as user. Every instruction sentence is moved, never reworded: the old single message builders stay in tree byte identical and the head plus the body hold every sentence they held. Retries keep the single user turn the code always rebuilt, with the feedback in it. The mine payload dump gains `sort_keys=True`, so identical logical payloads hash and prefix alike.

Five tests on invented domains cover the head bytes identical across two Tasks and two attempts for each stage, the head plus body sentence cover against the old form, and the byte identical mine dump across insertion orders. `scripts/prompt_snapshot.py` builds both stages over ten invented inputs and checks the cover sentence for sentence. Both heads sit below the provider minimum (intent about 60 tokens, judge about 610 by word count times 1.3, the repo having no prompt tokenizer), so no live cache read is claimed: the live cache share is to be measured on the next launch.

### D250. Compile hold-out is by argument shape, and test_body faces that split (2026-09-11)

D51 asks every stage to be tested on what it did not see. The compile split did not do that. `split_calls` took every third recorded call by list index, so held-out was usually another call of the same argument shape: a reconstruction that matched the shown nested keys passed shown replay and held-out. `test_body` gated on shown only, with an empty held-out list, so the drafter submitted a body it had already marked passed. The class is a write whose option keys differ across calls, or a search whose result set size is an arity the shown calls never carried.

The rule. Membership of the split is the argument-shape hash: sorted key paths of args, nested dict keys, list lengths, values dropped. Shown and held-out then disagree on option keys, arities and nested key sets. Values stay withheld from the writer. `test_body` runs the same split and still reports a withheld count rather than hidden args, so a reconstruction that matches shown keys and misses a held-out key set fails before submit. When every recorded call of a tool shares one shape, the split falls back to every third call by index and the build row records `no_shape_holdout`.

Tests on an invented kiln domain: two ware shapes, one held out, `test_body` names a held-out shape fail without quoting values; one shared shape falls back to every third. Existing one-shape split tests keep their shown and held-out ids. Not measured live.
